## Chapter 2: Architecture Styles for the Cloud Era {#ch2}

> A style is not a destination; it is a bet about which changes will come.


Chapter 1 left Encore with a list of driving characteristics (survive a 500× on-sale spike, stay available when load is worst, never sell the same seat twice) and a first cut of components such as Sale Gate and Seat Inventory. It also left one question deliberately unanswered: what overall *shape* should the system take? Should all those components live in one deployable program, or in many? Should they talk by calling one another directly, or by publishing events for whoever cares to listen? Questions like these are questions of **architecture style**. A style is a named, recurring arrangement of components and the connections between them, together with the rules about how they may be combined. "Layered," "microservices" and "event-driven" are all styles in this sense: each is less a blueprint than a family resemblance that many systems share. Mary Shaw and David Garlan cataloged the first such styles in the early 1990s. Their gift to the field was not any single style but a vocabulary. Before them, engineers could sense that two systems had the same shape without having words to say so; after them, they could name the shape, compare it with others and argue about its consequences.

Choosing a style is the largest decision we make about a system, because nearly everything else follows from it. It fixes how components are packaged into deployable units, how they talk to one another, and which characteristics come cheap and which come dear. A style that makes elasticity cheap, for example, often does so by spreading data across many machines, which makes keeping that data consistent more expensive.

That is why every style in this chapter comes with its costs written beside its benefits, and why the monolith, which spent a decade as a term of abuse, gets a fair hearing. There is no best style, only a best fit for a particular system at a particular moment. Style selection is therefore an economic decision before it is a technical one: we are deciding which costs we are willing to pay to buy the characteristics we need most. For Encore, paying extra to absorb a 500× on-sale spike is a sound purchase; paying the same price for a back-office report that runs once a night would be waste. And because the decision rests on what the business needs, it is made system by system, not once for the whole company, and it is revisited when the business changes, as it always does.

### 2.1 Foundations of Style Analysis

#### Two axes that classify styles {#two-axes-that-generate-the-catalog}

Open any catalog of architecture styles and it can look like a menagerie: layered, microkernel, microservices, event-driven, space-based, each with its own diagrams and its own devotees. The catalog becomes far less bewildering once we notice that most of its entries are answers to just two questions. The first question is about *partitioning*, that is, how the code is divided into parts. A system can be split *technically*, by layer, so that all presentation code lives together, all business logic together and all persistence code together. Or it can be split *by domain*, by business capability, so that everything to do with orders lives in one place, everything to do with inventory in another, and everything to do with payments in a third. The second question is about *deployment*: does the system ship as *one unit*, a single thing we build, release and run, or as *many* units that are released and run separately? Each question has two answers, so crossing them gives four quadrants, and nearly every named style finds a home in one of them:

{% include diagrams/fig-2-1.svg %}

*Figure 2.1 — The style catalog on its two generating axes. The top-left quadrant, distributed but technically partitioned, is nearly empty because it is nearly always a mistake: services sliced by layer must all change together, so you pay distribution's costs and collect none of its benefits. Event-driven sits on the line because its event processors can be sliced either way. Axes after Richards & Ford.*

That top-left quadrant deserves a closer look, because it is empty for a reason. Suppose Encore deployed its system as separate services but sliced them by layer: a presentation service, a business-logic service and a data service. Now imagine the change Chapter 1 used to test boundaries, adding presale codes for fan clubs. The new code needs a field on the purchase screen, a rule that checks the code, and a column that stores it, which means one small feature touches all three services. All three must be changed, tested and released in step, and every call between them now crosses a network. Encore would pay everything that distribution costs (network latency, partial failures, more machinery to operate) and receive none of what distribution is supposed to buy, which is the freedom to change and release one part without the others.

The industry built that empty quadrant anyway, at scale, in the 2000s, and called it service-oriented architecture (SOA). Its intentions were good: reuse shared business services across the enterprise, and connect old and new systems through standard interfaces. In practice, though, orchestration-driven SOA cut systems into technical strata. At the bottom sat "business services" shared by everything; above them, an enterprise service bus that carried and translated every message; above that, orchestration engines that strung services together into business processes. Each stratum was owned by a different team. So changing one business feature, say a new discount rule, meant changing a shared service, a bus mapping and an orchestration flow, and that meant coordinating five teams and a middleware committee. The architecture had made every change a cross-team project.

Parnas's criterion from Chapter 1 explains why this failed. Parnas argued that each module should hide a decision likely to change, so that when the decision changes, only that module changes. In a business system, the decisions most likely to change are business rules: how a discount is computed, who may buy during a presale, when a refund is allowed. A technical layer hides none of these. Instead, each rule is smeared across every stratum, a piece of it in the shared service, a piece in the bus, a piece in the orchestration, and each stratum belongs to a different team. A domain partition does the opposite: the rules about presales live inside the component that owns presales, so a change to those rules stays inside it.

Our field tends to repeat its history in new clothes, and this story is no exception. Today the clothes are microservices. A microservices estate sliced by layer, or one that routes every call through a shared orchestration hub, is SOA again, only in smaller containers. The services are smaller and the tooling is newer, but the shape of change is the same, and so is the cost. The lesson fits in a sentence: *partition by domain, or every change becomes a negotiation.*

#### Reading a style's ratings {#reading-a-styles-price-tag}

Later in this chapter, Figure 2.2 rates the six core styles from one to five stars on deployability, elasticity, fault tolerance, simplicity, cost and data consistency. These ratings are useful, but only if we read them the right way, and the right way is the way we read a used-car listing. The listing tells us truthfully that one car has better fuel economy than another, but it cannot tell us whether we will ever drive far enough for that to matter. The stars are the same: accurate about how the styles compare with one another, silent about your situation. Five stars for elasticity, the ability to grow and shrink with load, are no bargain for a system whose load never moves, because we would be paying in complexity for a capacity we never use. The ratings turn into decisions only when we set them against *your* driving characteristics from Chapter 1, the few qualities your system must have to succeed. For Encore, that means asking which stars matter for an on-sale spike and which matter for the steady traffic of the rest of the week.

The ratings of the distributed styles also carry a hidden cost, and it has a name. Behind every one of them sit the eight fallacies of distributed computing, the comfortable assumptions that engineers keep making about networks and keep being punished for: that the network is reliable (it is *not*), that latency is zero (it is *not*), that bandwidth is infinite (it is *not*), and so on through the list. The fallacies have outlived the technologies they were first written about, because they describe networks, not any particular product. Chapter 4 treats them properly. Until then, a working rule will serve: every arrow that crosses a process boundary is a network call, so it costs latency and adds a new way for the system to fail, and that holds whether or not anyone drew the arrow on the diagram. When Encore's Order component asks Seat Inventory about a seat inside one process, that request is just a function call; if the two ran as separate services, the same request could be slow, could time out, or could succeed on one side while the other side never hears the answer.

**Recap.** Two axes, how code is partitioned and how it is deployed, generate most of the style catalog. SOA's failure taught the industry to partition by domain, because only domain boundaries hide the decisions that actually change, the business rules; layers smear those rules across teams. Star ratings are relative prices: they show how styles compare with one another, and they mean something only when weighed against your own driving characteristics, remembering that every distributed rating carries the cost of the network.

**Exercise 2.1.** Take the last three systems you worked on and place each in one of Figure 2.1's quadrants, writing one sentence per system that justifies its position on each axis. If any sat in the top left, distributed but technically partitioned, pick one recent feature and estimate what the layering cost: how many services or teams that feature had to touch, and how much coordination it took to release.

### 2.2 Monolithic Styles Done Right

#### Layered architecture and the sinkhole anti-pattern {#the-layered-default-and-its-sinkhole}

We begin where most systems begin. The layered monolith stacks three layers in one deployable: presentation on top (the screens and the web endpoints), business logic in the middle, and persistence at the bottom (the code that reads and writes the database). Each layer calls only the one beneath it. Most systems start this way because it matches how a single team naturally thinks about its work: someone builds the pages, someone writes the rules, someone looks after the database, and each concern has an obvious drawer to live in.

The idea has a distinguished lineage. In 1968 Edsger Dijkstra described the THE multiprogramming system, built at the Technische Hogeschool Eindhoven, as a stack of levels. Each level hid some messy reality of the hardware and presented a simpler machine to the level above, so the programs at each level could be reasoned about without knowing how the levels below were built. Our presentation, business and persistence layers are distant descendants of that idea: the business layer works with "orders" and "seats" and need not care how rows are stored.

The style's ratings follow from its shape. Simplicity earns ★★★★★, because there is one codebase, one deployment and one obvious place for everything. Cost earns ★★★★★, because there is one process to run and no distributed infrastructure to pay for. Data consistency earns ★★★★★, because everything shares one database, so a single transaction can update an order and its seats together. Elasticity earns only ★★: the system can scale only as a whole, so if Encore's checkout were drowning during an on-sale, we would have to copy the entire application, organizer dashboards and all, just to add checkout capacity.

The layered style's typical failure has a name: the *architecture sinkhole*. A sinkhole is a request that passes through every layer without any layer doing real work. Picture a fan opening an event's detail page on Encore. The presentation layer receives the request and calls the business layer; the business layer has no rule to apply, so it simply forwards the call to the persistence layer; the persistence layer runs one query and hands the row back up the stack, unchanged at every step. Three layers have each added code, tests and a little latency, and none has added a decision. One such path is harmless, since some requests are simply simple. But when most requests look like this, the layers have stopped earning their keep and become ceremony: we pay for structure that protects nothing. A common remedy is to mark some layers *open*, so that a request with no business logic may skip them, but a system riddled with sinkholes is usually telling us that layers were the wrong way to divide it.

#### The modular monolith {#the-modular-monolith-one-deployment-real-boundaries}

The modular monolith keeps the single deployment and changes the direction of the cut. Instead of slicing the code into technical layers, it partitions the code by *domain*, into modules such as Catalog, Orders and Inventory, all inside one deployable unit. Two rules make the modules real rather than nominal. First, each module owns its own tables, and no other module touches them. Second, modules talk to each other only through declared interfaces, the small set of operations each module chooses to publish. If the Orders module needs to hold seats, it calls the operation that Inventory publishes for holding seats; it does not open Inventory's seat table and update it directly.

These are the same rules microservices live by, and the difference lies in what enforces them. Between microservices, the network does the enforcing almost for free: one service physically cannot call a private function inside another, and if each has its own database it cannot quietly read the other's tables either. Inside a single process, nothing physical stops one module from calling into another's internals, so the compiler and the build system must take over the job the network would otherwise do. In return, a call between modules is an ordinary in-process function call, measured in nanoseconds instead of milliseconds, and there are no service meshes, message brokers or extra deployments to run. We get domain boundaries without paying distribution's price.

An analogy helps. Think of an apartment building. The units share a foundation, a roof and the utilities, yet the walls between units are real: your neighbor cannot walk into your kitchen, and a fire in one unit should not spread to the next. Microservices, by contrast, are a street of detached houses, each with its own foundation and its own plumbing, more independent but far more expensive to build and maintain. The analogy breaks at a telling point. A concrete wall stays standing with no one watching it, whereas a wall between modules is made of nothing but agreement. It stands only as long as something keeps checking it, because the language itself will happily let any code call any other.

That is why the hard part of a modular monolith is enforcement. Any module is one careless import away from reaching into another. A developer in a hurry adds a line that lets Orders query Inventory's seat table directly, the build passes, the feature ships, and the boundary is gone without anyone deciding to remove it. Repeat that a few hundred times and the modules exist only in the architecture diagram. The defense is Chapter 1's fitness functions, automated checks that the architecture still has the property we want. Each boundary rule becomes a check that runs on every change, and the most important ones fail the build. The fourth rule below concerns each module's public application programming interface (API), the operations it deliberately exposes to the others:

| Boundary rule | Enforced by |
|---|---|
| Module A may not import module B's internals | a dependency-analysis test in continuous integration (CI) that fails the build on a forbidden import |
| No module reads another module's tables | declared schema ownership, checked by SQL lint in CI |
| No dependency cycles between modules | a cycle-detection fitness function |
| Public module APIs are explicit | build-system visibility, so only exported packages can be called from outside |

None of this needs to be built from scratch, because off-the-shelf tools cover most stacks: ArchUnit or Spring Modulith on the Java Virtual Machine (JVM), NetArchTest in .NET, Packwerk in Ruby, import-linter in Python, and dependency-cruiser for JavaScript and TypeScript. Each lets a team write rules such as "nothing outside Inventory may use Inventory's internal packages" and turn a violation into a failed build. Their real value is that they run on every commit, long after the people who drew the boundaries have moved on to other teams or other companies. Good intentions leave with the people who held them, while an automated check stays and keeps saying no. *A modular monolith without automated boundary enforcement is an ordinary monolith with better intentions.*

#### Microkernel and pipeline architectures {#microkernel-and-pipeline-shapes-for-specific-problems}

Two more monolithic styles fit narrower problems, and fit them well. The **microkernel** consists of a minimal core plus plug-ins. The core provides the few services every feature needs and defines a contract that extensions must follow; the plug-ins supply the features, and new ones can be added without changing the core. The name comes from operating systems, where designers have argued for decades about how little belongs in the kernel and how much can live outside it as separate services. In applications the style suits products built around extension: integrated development environments (IDEs) with their plug-in ecosystems, browsers with their extensions, payment processors whose rules differ by country, and marketplaces of add-ons. A payment processor, for instance, can keep a stable core that moves money and add one plug-in per country, each carrying that country's local rules, so entering a new market means writing a plug-in rather than reopening the core.

The **pipeline** consists of filters connected by pipes. Each filter takes data in, transforms it, and passes the result on to the next. It suits transformation work: extract, transform, load (ETL) jobs that move data between systems, media processing that decodes, resizes and re-encodes, and compiler-like flows that turn source text into tokens, then into a syntax tree, then into code. Its best-known form is the Unix pipe, added to Unix in 1973 at Doug McIlroy's urging, which let small programs that each did one thing be joined into larger ones on the command line. The secret of the style is deliberate ignorance. Each filter knows only the shape of its input and the shape of its output; it does not know who produced the data or who will consume it. Because a filter assumes nothing about its neighbors, it can be reused in any pipeline that feeds it the right input.

The lesson that unites these styles is that every problem has a shape of its own, and the style should echo it. A product whose essence is extension wants a microkernel; a product whose essence is a flow of transformations wants a pipeline. Force the wrong style on a problem and the system spends its life fighting its own domain. A marketplace product forced into layers has no natural home for third-party add-ons, so each one gets wired in by hand. A workflow product forced into plug-ins scatters a single sequence of steps across components that were designed not to know about one another.

**Recap.** The layered style is a reasonable place to begin and a poor place to stay. Sinkholes, requests that pass through every layer while none does any work, are the sign that layers no longer earn their keep. The modular monolith partitions by domain inside a single deployment, giving us real boundaries without the cost of distribution, but only if automated checks enforce those boundaries on every commit. The microkernel suits products built around extension, and the pipeline suits work that is a flow of transformations.

**Exercise 2.2.** Sketch Encore's eight components (Section 1.3) as modules of a modular monolith, showing which module owns which data and which modules call which. Then choose the two module boundaries you would enforce first, explain why a breach of each would be most damaging, and name the check you would use for each (for example, a rule from the table above and the tool that would run it).

### 2.3 Distributed Styles

#### Service-based architecture {#service-based-the-pragmatic-middle}

The monolithic styles of Section 2.2 ship as a single unit; every style in this section ships as many, and the first of them is also the most modest. Service-based architecture splits the system into a handful of coarse domain services, typically 4 to 12, and those services usually still share one database. "Coarse" is the important word. Each service covers a whole business domain, such as everything to do with orders or everything to do with the event catalog, rather than one narrow function, so there are few services and few network boundaries between them. In my judgment it is the most underrated style in the catalog, because it gathers most of the benefits of distribution while declining most of the costs. Each major domain becomes its own deployable unit, so the team can ship a change to ordering without redeploying the catalog, and a crash in one service leaves the others serving. Yet because the services share a database, the system keeps the consistency that one database gives for free (a single transaction can still update related rows together), and a handful of services is something one operations team can comfortably run. Picture a town of a few districts. Each district can tear down and rebuild its own streets on its own schedule, without asking the others, but all of them draw on one water main.

That shared database is the water main, and like a water main it is both the town's convenience and its vulnerability. If the database goes down, every service goes down with it, so it remains a single point of failure. And because every service reads and writes the same tables, a change to one table's schema can break a service that was not supposed to be involved, which means every schema change must be checked against all of them. With four services this is a manageable conversation; with twelve, it becomes a standing meeting, so watch the database closely as the number of services grows. Even so, the style's ratings (elasticity ★★★, deployability ★★★★, cost ★★★★, data consistency ★★★★★) are remarkably balanced: across the whole of Figure 2.2 below, none of its ratings falls below three stars. That balance is what makes service-based architecture the right first distributed step for many systems, a place to learn what distribution costs before committing to more of it.

#### Microservices {#microservices-maximum-decoupling-maximum-invoice}

Microservices take domain partitioning as far as it will go. Instead of a handful of coarse services we get many small ones, and each owns its data outright: no other service may touch its database, and each deploys on its own schedule. Look at the ratings and a pattern appears. Every rating that concerns independence (deployability, elasticity, fault tolerance) rises to five stars, while simplicity and cost fall to one. These are not two separate facts but one fact seen from both ends: the falling ratings are the price of the rising ones. Independence is paid for with network calls where there used to be function calls, with data spread across many databases that no single transaction can span, and with the operational machinery (deployment pipelines, service discovery, monitoring and tracing) needed to keep dozens of moving parts visible and running.

So when is the price worth paying? The real prerequisite is not technical but organizational: many teams that need to move without coordinating with one another. Conway's law, from Chapter 1, tells us that a system tends to copy the communication structure of the organization that builds it, and microservices are what that structure looks like when there are many autonomous teams, each owning its own services. The need for that autonomy comes from arithmetic. As Chapter 5 explores through Brooks, the number of communication paths in a group grows much faster than the group itself, because every person must stay in step with every other. That growth is what eventually makes coordination expensive enough to be worth avoiding. But the arithmetic cuts both ways. Nine people, the size of Encore's whole engineering team, have only 36 pairs between them, few enough that a daily standup keeps everyone in step. A team of nine therefore has no coordination problem large enough to justify microservices' price; it would be paying for independence it already has. Chapter 5 covers the style in full. The short version is this: *microservices solve a scaling problem you measure in teams.*

#### Event-driven and space-based architectures {#event-driven-and-space-based-styles-for-motion-and-spikes}

Event-driven architecture connects components with *events* instead of calls. In a call, one component asks another to do something and waits for the answer. In an event-driven system, a producer announces a fact that has already happened, such as `OrderPlaced`, and any consumer that cares reacts in its own time; the producer waits for no one and need not even know who is listening. Biology offers a precise analogy. A nerve carries a signal to one particular destination, much as a call does. A hormone works differently: a gland releases it into the bloodstream, every cell with the right receptor responds, and the gland never learns who listened. Event-driven systems are endocrine in this sense. The analogy also predicts their characteristic difficulty. Hormonal disorders are notoriously hard to diagnose because cause and symptom may sit in different organs, and in the same way a wrong total on an organizer's dashboard may trace back to an event published, or missed, in a component far away.

The style comes in two topologies, which differ in who controls the flow of work. In the **broker** topology there is no coordinator at all. Events pass from processor to processor through a message broker, and each processor simply reacts to what it receives and perhaps publishes new events of its own. When an order is placed, Notification sends the ticket, Sales Analytics updates the dashboards and Bot Screening learns from the purchase, and none of them knows the others exist. This maximizes decoupling, since any processor can be added or removed without touching the rest, but it hides the end-to-end flow: the overall process exists only as the sum of many independent reactions, written down nowhere. In the **mediator** topology, a coordinator drives the workflow, telling each step when to run and noticing when one fails. That buys visibility and control, because the whole process can be read in one place, at the cost of a central hub that every workflow depends on and that must itself be kept simple and kept running.

Fault tolerance and elasticity are excellent, because a slow or failed consumer does not hold up the producer, and each consumer can be scaled independently to match its own load. The difficulty is understanding. Answering a simple question like *"what happens after X?"* means tracing events through several consumers, each of which may publish events of its own, and no single piece of code tells the whole story. Chapter 6 is about doing this well.

Space-based architecture attacks a different problem: the database as a bottleneck. In most styles, every write eventually waits on one database, and under enough load that database is the first thing to saturate. Space-based architecture moves the working state out of the database and into memory. Processing units keep the data they need in an in-memory grid that is replicated across many units, so they read and write at memory speed and the system grows by adding units. The database still exists, but it becomes an eventually updated system of record: changes reach it asynchronously, after the fact, rather than as part of each request. The style exists for extreme, spiky, write-heavy load such as flash sales, auctions and betting, which is exactly the shape of an on-sale. Its price is the hardest data-consistency reasoning in the catalog. Suppose two fans, served by two different processing units, each try to buy the last seat in the same moment. Each unit sees the seat as free in its own replica of the grid, and the two replicas now disagree. In a conventional system the database would settle the matter, letting one transaction commit and rejecting the other. Here no database stands by to settle it in time, and the design itself must ensure that the seat is sold exactly once.

#### Serverless and cell-based architectures {#serverless-and-cells-cloud-native-shapes}

Two shapes born in the cloud cut across the catalog. They are less about how to partition a system than about how to run and contain whatever partitioning we choose. **Serverless** (functions plus fully managed services such as hosted queues and databases) is better understood as a cost curve than as a partitioning. You pay per invocation and nothing at all while idle, and the system scales as fast as the platform can start new instances, with billing metered by the millisecond. For a workload that is quiet most of the day and frantic for ten minutes, that curve is very attractive. The fine print matters, though. A function that has not run recently suffers a *cold start*, a delay while the platform prepares a fresh instance; each invocation has an execution time limit; and functions keep no state between invocations, so anything that must be remembered lives in an external store. Serverless therefore suits spiky, event-shaped work, short tasks triggered by something happening, and fits poorly with long-running or stateful work. Chapter 11 weighs it against containers.

**Cell-based architecture** runs several complete, independent copies of a system, called cells, each serving a fixed slice of users. Imagine an Encore split this way, with every cell holding its own full set of components and its own data for its own share of fans. A bad deploy or a runaway workload then takes down one cell and leaves the others running, so a failure that would have hit every fan hits only some of them. A ship's watertight bulkheads work on the same principle: the hull is divided into compartments, so a breach floods one compartment instead of the whole ship. The analogy also warns us where the protection ends. Bulkheads help only if the water cannot find a way over or around them, and cells protect only if nothing they share, such as the routing layer that sends each user to a cell or the control plane that deploys and configures them, can carry a failure from one cell to all the others. Cells are not an alternative to the styles above. They limit the *blast radius* of a failure, the share of users it can reach, on top of whatever style each cell uses inside. Chapter 14 builds on them.

#### The styles, side by side

| | Layered | Modular monolith | Service-based | Microservices | Event-driven | Space-based |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Deployability | ★★ | ★★★ | ★★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★★ | ★★★ |
| Elasticity | ★★ | ★★ | ★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★★★★ |
| Fault tolerance | ★ | ★★ | ★★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★★★ |
| Simplicity | ★★★★★ | ★★★★ | ★★★ | ★ | ★★ | ★ |
| Cost | ★★★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★★★ | ★ | ★★★ | ★★ |
| Data consistency | ★★★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★★★★ | ★★ | ★★ | ★★ |

*Figure 2.2 — Relative ratings of the six core styles. Compare whole columns rather than single rows: no column is all stars, which is Chapter 1's first law of software architecture, that everything is a trade-off, set out as a table. Ratings adapted from Richards & Ford; the data-consistency row and several cells are this book's own judgment.*

**Recap.** Service-based architecture is the balanced middle: a few coarse services that deploy and fail independently while sharing one database. Microservices buy independence for many teams at the highest operational price, a price worth paying only when those teams actually exist. Event-driven architecture trades end-to-end visibility for decoupling, and space-based architecture trades simple consistency for the ability to survive spikes. Serverless and cells are not partitionings at all but a cost curve and a limit on blast radius, laid over whichever style we choose.

**Exercise 2.3.** Using Figure 2.2 and Encore's seven characteristics from Chapter 1, eliminate three of the six styles for Encore *before* reading Section 2.4. Write one sentence per elimination, naming the characteristic and the star rating that rules the style out.

### 2.4 Choosing a Style

#### Encore's style decision, worked through {#the-decision-worked-honestly}

We now have a catalog of styles and a price for each, and it is time to choose one for Encore. Choosing a style is the largest decision we will make about the system, but it needs no new method. It uses the one from Chapter 1: state the options fairly, set down what each one costs, and let the characteristics the business committed to decide between them. The only difference is scale. Instead of choosing between a queue and a topic, we are choosing the shape of everything.

Five questions, which we will call drivers, do most of the deciding. The first is **domain shape**: how many naturally separate business capabilities does the system have? A system with two capabilities gains little from being cut into many pieces. The second is **team topology**: how many teams must be able to change and ship their part without waiting for one another? This is the question that justifies or rules out the most distributed styles. The third is **operational maturity**: who carries the pager when something breaks at 3 a.m., and how skilled are they at running distributed systems? A style the team cannot operate is a style it cannot have. The fourth is **characteristic outliers**: are there components whose needs differ sharply from the rest, so that one part needs a five-star rating where everything else is content with two? The fifth is the **cost ceiling**: what can the business afford to spend running the system, every day, whether or not anything interesting is happening?

Encore's answers to the first questions eliminate styles quickly. Encore has nine engineers on one team. As Section 2.3 showed, nine people have only 36 pairwise conversations between them, which one daily standup covers, so microservices would solve a coordination problem Encore does not have, while charging their full operational price. Encore's steady traffic is also modest. Space-based architecture keeps replicated in-memory grids running so that it is always ready for a flood of writes, and those grids cost money every hour of every day. For Encore they would sit mostly idle, so the style would charge daily and pay off only on the days of a major on-sale.

And yet one outlier stands out: the on-sale spike. When a top artist releases tickets, traffic jumps 500× in minutes, and every fan in that crowd must pass through Sale Gate. Sale Gate therefore needs elasticity ★★★★★, while the rest of the system, from Event Catalog to the organizer dashboards, is content with ★★. If we insist on one style for the whole system, we face an unpleasant choice. A single modular monolith scales only as a whole, so it would either starve Sale Gate, leaving it with the modest elasticity everything else gets, or force us to scale every component up for the spike, making the organizer dashboards elastic enough for a stampede they will never see. Choose a fully elastic style instead, and we pay its price in cost and simplicity across all eight components to serve the needs of one.

The way out is to notice that a style decision does not have to cover the whole system at once. It can be made part by part, as long as we know what counts as a part. The right unit is the **architecture quantum**, a term coined in *Building Evolutionary Architectures* and sharpened in *Software Architecture: The Hard Parts*. An architecture quantum is the smallest part of a system that can be deployed on its own and still do useful work. It carries its own code, its own data and therefore its own characteristics. Inside, it is highly cohesive: its pieces belong together and change together. Outside, it has no synchronous dependencies, meaning it never has to call another part of the system and wait for the answer before it can do its job. That last condition is what makes a quantum truly independent. If Sale Gate had to wait on a call into the monolith for every fan in the queue, it could never be more elastic than the monolith it waits on, however many copies of Sale Gate we ran.

Once we see the system as a set of quanta, a familiar question changes shape. Characteristics belong to quanta, not to systems. "How elastic is Encore?" has no single answer; the useful question is "how elastic is *which* quantum?" The ratings, the drivers and the selection method all apply to each quantum separately. So one system can contain a modular-monolith quantum and a serverless quantum, each judged against its own scorecard of ratings, each paying only the price its own needs justify.

A hospital works this way. The wards are planned for a steady, predictable load of patients. The emergency department has its own entrance, its own triage process and its own staffing model, because it is designed for surges that arrive without warning. Nobody rebuilds the maternity ward to emergency-department standards just because the hospital, taken as a whole, sometimes has to cope with a multi-vehicle crash. The analogy breaks at staffing, and the break is instructive. A hospital's surge capacity is people and beds, which take months to hire and build. Encore's surge capacity is compute, rented by the minute and handed back when the crowd has gone. That is why carving out a surge quantum is so attractive in the cloud: the emergency department can grow to meet the crash and shrink again before the bill gets large. Here is Encore's answer:

{% include diagrams/fig-2-3.svg %}

*Figure 2.3 — Encore's chosen shape: a modular monolith serves the steady majority of components, and the on-sale quantum (Sale Gate and Bot Screening) is extracted into its own deployment and scaled independently. The one characteristic outlier, elasticity, drove the style decision.*

The architecture decision record (ADR) for this choice is honest about what it costs. Its consequences row says that Encore accepts a network boundary around exactly one quantum, and with it the fallacies of distributed computing that Section 2.1 introduced: calls across that boundary now cost latency and can fail in ways an in-process call cannot. In return, Encore can scale that quantum 500× without paying to scale the other six components. One network boundary, deliberately placed, is a far smaller bill than the many boundaries a fully distributed style would bring, each with the same fallacies.

The membership of the quantum is worth a second look, because it is not decided by the "Driven by" column of Chapter 1's component table. Bot Screening sits in the on-sale quantum even though security, not elasticity, is what drives it. The reason is load. Every fan is screened before Sale Gate admits them, so Bot Screening meets the full spike at the same moment Sale Gate does. Leave it in the monolith and Sale Gate would have to call across the boundary, and wait, for every fan in a crowd 500 times the usual size, which is exactly the synchronous dependency a quantum must not have. The rule that falls out is simple. Take the founders' sentence, "When a top artist drops tickets, traffic is 500× normal for 10 minutes," and lay it over the component list. The components that stand between the raw crowd and admission, the queue and the bot check, must each handle every fan in it, so they form the quantum. The purchase path after admission sees only the metered flow Sale Gate lets through, at the rate Seat Inventory can sustain, and can stay in the monolith with the other components. The boundary falls where the business's own words say it should.

**Recap.** Five drivers guide the choice of style: domain shape, team topology, operational maturity, characteristic outliers and cost ceiling. Style is chosen per architecture quantum, the smallest independently deployable part of a system with no synchronous dependencies outside it, so the steady parts of a system need not pay for the volatile ones. Encore keeps a modular monolith for the steady majority and extracts only the on-sale quantum, Sale Gate and Bot Screening, accepting one network boundary so that it can scale that quantum alone.

**Exercise 2.4.** Write Encore's style ADR, the decision shown in Figure 2.3, in the Chapter 1 format: title, status, context, decision, consequences and Governance. The consequences row must name at least two real losses, things that become worse, harder or more expensive because of this decision, stated as plainly as the gains.

<h3 id="25-styles-are-bets--hedge-them">2.5 Hedging Style Decisions</h3>

We have now chosen a style for Encore, and it is worth pausing to admit what kind of choice that was. Every style is a bet about which changes will come. When we picked a modular monolith with one extracted quantum, we were wagering that Encore would stay a single team of nine for a while, that its steady traffic would stay modest, and that the on-sale spike would remain the one outlier. Those are reasonable bets, but they are still bets, and the future has a habit of collecting on the ones we were most sure of. A prudent architect does what a prudent investor does: places the bet, and then hedges it, so that being wrong costs a repair rather than a rewrite.

The first and most valuable hedge is to keep domain boundaries clean *inside* whatever you build. Consider what happens if, two years from now, Encore needs to pull Orders & Payments out of the monolith into its own service. In a modular monolith, Orders & Payments already owns its tables and is reached only through a declared interface, so the split is mostly a matter of putting a network call where an in-process call used to be. In a tangled monolith, where any code may read any table and call any function, nobody can even say where Orders & Payments ends; the split begins with months of archaeology. A clean boundary does not make the split free. A transaction that today updates an order and a seat hold together, in one database commit, has to be unwound into a coordinated sequence of steps once the two live in different services, and Chapter 9 shows how. But the boundary turns an open-ended excavation into a known, bounded piece of work.

Clean boundaries do not stay clean on their own, which is why the second hedge is to automate the boundary checks. As Section 2.2 argued, a wall between modules stands only as long as something keeps checking it, because good intentions erode: a deadline arrives, someone imports a class from the wrong module because it is right there, and the shortcut becomes a dependency that the next person builds on. A check that runs on every commit and fails the build when Catalog reaches into Inventory's tables catches that shortcut on the day it is written, when it costs a few minutes to fix, instead of on the day of the split, when it costs weeks.

It helps to think of a clean boundary in the language of finance, as an option bought in advance. An option is the right, but not the obligation, to do something later at a price agreed today. Encore pays a small, steady premium for this one: a little discipline in code review, and a build check that occasionally says no. In return it holds the right to split a module out cheaply whenever it chooses, and it is never obliged to exercise that right. If the day never comes, the premium was modest. If it does come, the option pays for itself many times over, because the alternative is to buy the same separation at the moment you need it most, under deadline, in a codebase that has spent years growing together. That is the whole asymmetry: a boundary is cheap to hold and dear to buy late.

The third hedge is to treat the style decision as something we revisit rather than something we settle once. A decision is only as good as the inputs it was made from, so re-run it when those inputs change. Three changes matter most. The first is that the team count doubles: Encore's nine engineers on one team become two or three teams that want to ship without waiting for each other, and the team-topology driver that ruled out microservices begins to point the other way. The second is that a new outlier characteristic appears: some component starts to need ratings very different from its neighbors, as Sale Gate's elasticity did, and so becomes a candidate for its own quantum. The third is that the company makes an acquisition: Encore buys, say, a merchandise business with its own system and its own team, and the domain shape changes overnight. None of these events means the original decision was wrong. They mean it was made for a company that no longer quite exists.

When the decision does need to change, it rarely needs to leap. There is a well-worn path: from a monolith, to a modular monolith, to service-based architecture, and perhaps, eventually, to microservices. Each step on that path buys more independence and costs more in network calls, distributed data and operations. The virtue of the path is that it lets each step wait until a real pressure demands it and pays for it. Encore should not move from a modular monolith to service-based architecture because service-based is the next rung on the ladder; it should move when something it can measure, such as teams blocking one another's releases, makes the current rung too expensive to stay on.

There is an old observation behind this habit of growing systems in steps. John Gall noted in *Systemantics* (1975) that a complex system that works has always grown from a simple system that worked, and that a complex system designed from scratch never works and cannot be patched up to make it work; you have to start over with a simple system that works. Gall offered this as a wry law of how systems behave. The migration path takes his observation and turns it into a plan: start with the simplest style that serves today's drivers, make it work, and let each later step add complexity only on top of something already proven to run.

> **The résumé trap.** The most common style-selection error is optimizing for the architect's next job instead of the business's next year. Microservices, event streams and service meshes look good on a résumé, and having run a system built on them is a fine line to put there. But if your justification cites companies a thousand times your size, you are imitating companies whose problems you do not have. Their architecture answers their drivers, such as hundreds of teams or traffic that never sleeps; Encore's should answer Encore's.

**Recap.** Hedge the style decision three ways. Keep domain boundaries clean inside whatever you build, so a later split is bounded work rather than an excavation. Automate the checks that guard those boundaries, so they survive deadlines and turnover. And re-run the decision when its inputs change, such as the team count, a new outlier or an acquisition. Take each step along the migration path only when a measured pressure demands it, and justify a style with your own drivers, not someone else's.

**Exercise 2.5.** Take the ADR you wrote in Exercise 2.4 and list three events that would force Encore to re-run the style decision (for example, the team count doubling). For each event, write down two things: the metric or signal that would tell you it has happened (what you would actually observe, and where), and the style change it would most likely trigger.

### Kata: Style Selection — Three Briefs

Chapter 1's kata asked us to apply one method to one brief. This one asks for something harder: to apply the same method to three briefs and let it lead somewhere different each time. The three briefs below are set side by side on purpose, because each one has a different correct answer. If you find yourself choosing the same style three times, something other than the characteristics is doing the choosing, and the exercise has caught it. For each brief, work through the same four deliverables. First, name its driving characteristics, no more than seven, so that you are forced to rank them rather than list everything that sounds desirable. Second, choose a style for each quantum, remembering from Section 2.4 that a system may contain more than one. Third, draw a diagram at the level of detail of Figure 2.3: the quanta, the style of each, and the boundary between them. Fourth, write one ADR that records the decision and, above all, what it costs.

> **Kata A: "Ledgerline."** Business-to-business (B2B) invoicing, sold as software as a service (SaaS). It serves 40,000 small-business customers, with a steady load during working hours. A five-person team builds and runs it, and its revenue depends on being *boringly* reliable. The data needs finance-grade consistency: an invoice total or a payment status must never be wrong, even briefly. The chief technology officer (CTO) sums it up: "we sell trust, not fireworks."

> **Kata B: "Pulse."** A live audience-reaction app for TV shows. For 20 hours a day it has no traffic at all; then, when a host says "vote now," it has 2 million concurrent users for 90 seconds. A team of 12 engineers builds it. Losing 2% of votes is acceptable; being slow is fatal.

> **Kata C: "Argus."** An analytics product that ingests sensor feeds from retail stores continuously, while its customers run heavy ad-hoc queries over that data each morning. Data volume grows 10× a year, and the ingest must never pause. Three teams, 20 engineers in all, build it.

Read the briefs the way we read Encore's founders' sentences in Chapter 1: each phrase is a characteristic in disguise. "Boringly reliable" and "finance-grade consistency" point Ledgerline toward the simple, consistent end of the ratings table, and a five-person team is a hard limit on how much operations it can afford. Pulse's 20 idle hours and 90-second stampede are the characteristic outlier in its purest form, and its tolerance for losing 2% of votes tells you which consistency guarantees you may give up to buy speed. A slow answer is worse than a lost vote, presumably because a vote that lands after the 90 seconds are over might as well never have been cast. Argus has two workloads with opposite shapes, a ceaseless stream in and a heavy burst of queries out, and three teams who need to move without waiting on one another. Its rule that the ingest must never pause suggests that a reading missed while the pipeline is down cannot simply be fetched again later. Those clues do not hand you the answer, but they tell you where to look for it.

**Rubric:**

| Criterion | The question to ask yourself |
|---|---|
| Arbitration | Did the driving characteristics decide, rather than the star ratings in the table alone? |
| Quantum thinking | Did you extract the outliers into their own quanta, rather than upgrading the whole system to suit them? |
| Economics | Can the team that will pay the operations bill actually carry it, in people, skills and money? |
| The stress test | Halfway through, each brief doubles its team and gains one new characteristic. Ledgerline must keep EU customers' data in the EU; Pulse must prove that every counted vote was cast by a real viewer; and Argus's biggest customer demands queries over data less than a minute old. Does your style survive the change, or did it quietly assume nothing would change? |

The stress test deserves a word of explanation, because it is the row that tests the style rather than the brief. Section 2.5 argued that every style is a bet about which changes will come. The stress test is that bet coming due. A design that survives it usually needs adjustment rather than demolition: a new quantum carved out along a boundary that was already clean, say, or a characteristic re-rated for one part of the system. A design that does not survive it was usually tuned to the brief as written, with no room left for the business to change its mind.

#### Where you now stand

You can now name the styles, price them, and choose one per quantum, with the characteristics acting as arbiter whenever the options pull in different directions. What you cannot yet do is draw the *internal* boundaries that make any of these styles work: where one module ends and the next begins, and why the line falls there rather than somewhere else. A modular monolith is only as good as its modules, and a service-based system only as good as the cut between its services. Where those lines belong is less a technical question than a question about the business itself, and the discipline that answers it, domain-driven design, is the subject of Chapter 3.
