## Chapter 4: Distributed Systems Foundations for Architects {#ch4}

> Distribution is not an upgrade; it is a mortgage. This chapter is the amortization table.


The last two chapters bought Encore real freedom. Together, extracting Sale Gate in Chapter 2 and drawing context boundaries in Chapter 3 gave the company two things: teams that can move on their own schedules, and a front door that can scale on its own, apart from the rooms behind it. But nothing in architecture is free, and the bill for that freedom is a network. Components that once lived in the same process, calling each other as cheaply as calling a function, now talk across wires, and wires obey rules that no framework can repeal. A message can be lost on the way, so the seat request a fan sent never arrives. It can arrive twice, so the same reservation is attempted again. It can arrive late, after the system has already moved on. The clocks on two machines disagree, so neither can say with certainty which event happened first. And half of a system can die while the other half carries on, perfectly cheerful, still taking orders it can no longer complete. Leslie Lamport once defined a distributed system as one in which the failure of a computer you didn't even know existed can render your own computer unusable. Anyone who has carried a pager will recognize the joke for what it really is: not an exaggeration, but a specification of how these systems behave.

The network, then, brings a new kind of complexity, and the most useful way to sort it is the distinction Fred Brooks drew between essential and accidental complexity, which we met in Chapter 1. Some of the complexity of distribution is essential, because it comes from the problem itself. When a popular on-sale opens, Encore must absorb a 500× spike in traffic within minutes. No single machine can do that, so the work has to be spread across many machines, and the difficulties that come with many machines are part of selling tickets at that scale. Much of the remaining complexity, however, is accidental: we put it there ourselves. It is the residue of defaults nobody consciously chose, such as a timeout left at whatever the library shipped with, and of remote calls written as though they were local, as if asking another service across the network were as safe and quick as calling a method in the same program. The distinction matters because the two kinds call for different responses. Essential complexity has to be designed for; accidental complexity can be removed. Architects who cannot tell them apart build systems that work in demos, where traffic is light and the network is kind, and fail on Saturdays, when the big on-sale opens and every hidden assumption is tested at once.

None of this means an architect has to become a distributed systems researcher. You need not implement a consensus protocol yourself. What you must be able to do is narrower and more practical. You must predict how the system will behave at 10× its usual load with half the network gone, before that day arrives. You must read a vendor's claims about consistency with informed skepticism, knowing which guarantees are being offered and which are quietly left out. And you must design how the system fails in advance, choosing its failure behavior deliberately rather than discovering it during an incident. This chapter gives you the foundations for those three skills. Along the way, in Section 4.4, Encore suffers its first real outage, and like most outages it is self-inflicted: nothing dramatic fails, yet Encore's own components, each following its defaults, combine to take the site down.

### 4.1 The Physics of Distribution

#### The eight fallacies of distributed computing {#the-eight-famous-lies}

Every engineer who moves from one machine to many carries a set of assumptions across the border, and most of them turn out to be false. The best-known list of those false assumptions is the eight fallacies of distributed computing, which took shape at Sun Microsystems in the 1990s. Peter Deutsch is usually credited with the first seven, around 1994, building on notes by Bill Joy and Tom Lyon; James Gosling added the eighth a few years later. What makes the fallacies worth studying is that nobody believes them on purpose. Ask any engineer whether the network is reliable and they will laugh. They are errors of convenience: assumptions our programming models quietly invite us to make, because the code reads as though they were true.

The clearest example of such an invitation is the remote procedure call (RPC). In the classic form Andrew Birrell and Bruce Nelson gave it in 1984, an RPC makes a call to another machine look exactly like a call to a local function: you pass arguments, you get a result, and the network in between is hidden. It was a pleasant illusion, and a whole generation of middleware was built on it. In 1994 Jim Waldo and colleagues at Sun pushed back in "A Note on Distributed Computing." Their argument was that a remote object is not a local object that happens to live far away; it is a different kind of thing. A local call takes nanoseconds, and a remote one can take milliseconds or seconds. A local call either happens or the whole program crashes, while a remote call can fail halfway, leaving you unsure whether the other side acted at all. And a remote object is being used by other callers at the same moment. Hiding those differences behind a familiar syntax does not make them go away; it only postpones the moment you pay for them, usually to a busier and more expensive day. The lesson has not dated. A modern microservice, called through a generated client library that looks like an ordinary method on an ordinary object, is the same illusion in newer clothes.

So rather than treat the fallacies as a list of warnings to nod at, we read them as an invoice. Each line is a cost Encore pays whether or not it budgeted for it, and the only choice Encore has is whether to pay it by design or by surprise:

| The lie | What it costs Encore |
|---|---|
| The network is reliable | Every call from Sale Gate to Seat Inventory needs a deliberately chosen timeout and retry policy, because some calls will simply never get an answer |
| Latency is zero | A seat-map render that makes 30 sequential cross-region calls, each waiting for the last, spends its whole latency budget on the speed of light |
| Bandwidth is infinite | Shipping the full seating map on every update saturates the network during an on-sale, when thousands of fans are watching the same map change |
| The network is secure | The subject of Chapter 10 |
| Topology doesn't change | Deploys, autoscaling, and failovers reshuffle the addresses of Encore's services hourly, so nothing can rely on a fixed address |
| There is one administrator | The payment service provider's (PSP's) maintenance window is not on Encore's calendar, and Encore may learn of it only when payments slow down |
| Transport cost is zero | Serialization and Transport Layer Security (TLS) handshakes are real CPU work on the hot path, paid on every call |
| The network is homogeneous | A fan on overloaded stadium Wi-Fi is part of Encore's distributed system, just as much as Encore's own servers are |

*Figure 4.1 — The eight fallacies, each priced in Encore's terms. The first two, reliability and latency, drive most of this chapter.*

#### Time, ordering, and idempotency {#time-ordering-and-the-duty-of-idempotency}

Inside a single process, "before" and "after" are facts. One instruction runs, then the next, and the program can always say which came first. Across machines, those words turn into claims, and claims can conflict. Each server's clock drifts at its own rate, so two clocks that agreed this morning disagree by lunchtime. Messages take different routes through the network and can overtake one another. As a result, if Encore's Orders & Payments service logs a charge at 20:00:00.120 and Seat Inventory logs the seat's release at 20:00:00.115, those timestamps cannot tell us which really happened first; the five milliseconds between them may be nothing more than clock drift. Leslie Lamport made this point precise in his 1978 paper "Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System." His insight was that the only order we can trust across machines is causality: if one event sent a message and another event happened because that message arrived, the first event certainly came before the second. That order travels with the messages themselves. The wall clock, by comparison, is at best a hint.

A large orchestra lives with a version of this problem. Sound takes a perceptible moment to cross a wide stage, so a violinist at the front and a timpanist at the back hear each other slightly late. If every player kept time by ear, following whoever they could hear, the orchestra would gradually drift apart. So nobody keeps time by ear: everyone watches the conductor, whose beat reaches every player at the speed of light, effectively at the same instant. The conductor is a single source of order that everyone defers to. Software has its own conductors. A sequence number says "this is change 41, and that is change 42." A version counter lets a store reject an update made against an out-of-date copy. And single-writer ownership means that exactly one component is allowed to order the changes to a given piece of state, so the question "which change came first?" always has one authoritative answer. Chapter 3's aggregates, with one owner per invariant, already work this way: the seat's aggregate decides the order in which holds and releases apply to it. The analogy breaks, though, where software is harsher than music. A conductor does not freeze mid-phrase for several seconds and then resume, still convinced the orchestra is following. A server can, after a long pause, wake up believing it is still in charge while another has taken over. Section 4.2 shows what we do when ours does.

Ordering is one half of the problem; repetition is the other, and it must be designed for just as deliberately. A message that seems lost may only be late, so the sender sends it again, and now the receiver may see it twice. The defense is to make operations *idempotent*: an operation is idempotent when applying it twice leaves the system in the same state as applying it once. Setting a seat's status to "sold" is naturally idempotent, because setting it again changes nothing. Adding one to a counter is not, because every repetition adds another one.

> **The duty of idempotency.** Any operation that crosses a network will eventually be retried, whether by your own code after a timeout, by a proxy in the middle, or by a fan double-clicking "Pay," so it must be safe to apply twice. Consider how the request is phrased. "Charge the card for order 123" double-charges on a retry, because the second request looks like a new instruction to charge again. "Charge order 123's single charge-intent" does not, because both requests name the same intent; the receiver can see that this intent has already been executed and simply return the original result. Build idempotency into the first version. Retrofitting it later is far harder, because it means finding every caller that has been quietly assuming it was already there.

#### Tail latency {#tails-not-averages}

Averages comfort the people who compute them, but users do not experience averages; each user experiences one request, and the unlucky ones experience the slow end of the distribution, the tail. Jeffrey Dean and Luiz André Barroso named this problem "the tail at scale" in 2013, and the arithmetic behind it is unforgiving, because tails compound. Suppose a page has to call 10 services before it can render, and each service has a 1-in-100 chance of stalling for 2 seconds. Each call is fine 99% of the time, but the page is fine only if all 10 calls are fine, and the chance of that is 0.99 multiplied by itself 10 times, about 0.90. So the page stalls on roughly one load in 10. A slowdown that was rare for each service has become routine for the user. Now consider Encore's on-sales. A 99th-percentile latency (p99) of 2 s means one request in every hundred takes 2 seconds or longer, and at on-sale rates one in a hundred is thousands of fans watching spinners during the minute that matters most to them. That is why we set latency budgets at p99 rather than on the average, and why we treat every fan-out, every point where one request becomes many, as a multiplier on the tail.

A budget needs prices, or it is only a wish. A structural engineer knows the strength of steel without looking it up, and the orders of magnitude below are the material properties of our trade. They are worth memorizing, not to the decimal place, but well enough to notice when a design asks for something the materials cannot provide:

| Operation (2026) | Order of magnitude |
|---|---|
| Function call, same process | ~10 ns |
| Local solid-state drive (SSD) read | ~100 µs |
| Round trip, same availability zone (AZ) | 0.1–1 ms |
| Round trip, cross-region, same continent | 10–50 ms |
| Round trip, intercontinental | 70–250 ms |
| Object-store read (first byte) | 20–100 ms |
| Large language model (LLM) inference — first token / each token | 200–2,000 ms / 10–50 ms |

Read the table from top to bottom and the gaps are startling: a single round trip within one availability zone (one data center, roughly) costs as much as tens of thousands of in-process function calls, and a cross-region round trip costs tens to hundreds of times more again. The table also explains the earlier invoice line about 30 sequential cross-region calls: at 10–50 ms each, they add up to between a third of a second and a second and a half, before any real work is done. Hence Encore's rule: a seat-map render may spend a few same-AZ round trips, but never a cross-region one, and it never waits on a model. On an architecture diagram, an arrow between two boxes costs nothing to draw, and every arrow looks alike. This table is what each arrow costs to run.

**Recap.** The network breaks eight common assumptions, and Encore pays for each one: with timeouts and retry policies, with restraint in how much it sends, and with designs that expect addresses, administrators, and clients to be outside its control. Order across machines does not come for free from clocks; it must be designed, with sequence numbers, versions, and single owners. Every operation that might be retried must be idempotent from the first version. And latency is budgeted at p99, because fan-out quietly multiplies rare slowness into common slowness.

**Exercise 4.1.** Find one non-idempotent network operation in a system you know, such as a payment, an email notification, or a counter increment. Describe a concrete sequence of events that makes it fire twice (for example, a timeout followed by a retry), state what the user or the business sees as a result, and propose the smallest change that makes a second execution harmless.

### 4.2 State — Replication, Partitioning, Consistency

#### Replication and partitioning {#copies-and-slices}

Stateless code is the easy part of any system to spread across machines. If a request handler keeps nothing between calls, we can run ten copies of it or a thousand, and any copy can serve any fan. State is different. State is the part of a system that resists distribution, because the moment the same facts live on more than one machine, those machines must somehow agree on them. When we need more of a stateful system than one machine can give, we have only two moves available, and every distributed database is some combination of the two.

The first move is **replication**: we make complete copies of the data and keep them on several machines. Copies serve two purposes. They let the system survive machine death, since if one copy's disk fails another still holds the data. And they spread *reads*, since any copy can answer a question about the data. Encore's catalog is the textbook case. It is written about once a week, when a promoter adds a tour, yet on tour-announcement day it is read millions of times an hour by fans checking dates and venues. Writes are rare and reads are a flood, so we keep many copies and let each one take its share of the flood.

The second move is **partitioning**, also called **sharding**: instead of copying all the data, we cut it into slices and give each slice to a different machine. Replication cannot help when *writes* outgrow one machine, because every copy must absorb every write; ten copies means ten machines each doing all the work. Partitioning divides the work instead. The data is split by a *partition key*, a field in each record that decides which shard it lives on, and a good key does two jobs at once. It spreads the load evenly, so no shard does far more than its neighbors, and it keeps each request on one shard, so that a single operation never has to coordinate across machines.

A city library does both. It places a copy of the season's most popular novel in every branch, which is replication: many readers, each served nearby. And it sends the law collection to one building and the music scores to another, which is partitioning: each collection lives in exactly one place, and a lawyer never needs to visit the music library. The analogy also shows the danger in choosing a key. Encore keys seats by `event_id`, which keeps every operation on one concert's seats inside one shard, so reserving seat 14B never needs a conversation between machines. That is the fast path. The catch is that the *whole* load of an on-sale lands on that one shard too. When a stadium tour goes on sale, every fan in the queue wants seats for the same `event_id`, so they all arrive at the same shard at the same moment, like a whole town wanting the same book from the same branch on the same morning. Encore accepts this hot spot knowingly, because the Gate meters how many fans are admitted to the on-sale before they ever reach Seat Inventory, which keeps that one shard below what it can handle (Section 4.3 has the numbers).

Replication has a price of its own, and it is the gap between copies. In the most common arrangement, one copy, the *leader*, accepts writes and then passes them on to the other copies, the *followers*, a few milliseconds or seconds later. Walk through what that means for a fan. She buys two tickets; the leader records the order and acknowledges it, and her screen says "purchase confirmed." She taps "my tickets." That read happens to be served by a follower that has not yet received the new order, so the list comes back empty. Nothing has crashed and no data is lost; the follower simply has not caught up. But to the fan, who has just paid, an empty list looks exactly like a failure. Here the library analogy breaks, and the way it breaks is instructive. A borrower who finds the shelf empty can see that the book is out on loan, so the library at least tells the truth about what it lacks. A stale replica gives no such sign. It answers with an old version of the data, with complete confidence, and nothing in the reply warns the caller that a newer version exists.

#### Consistency models {#the-consistency-menu}

Since copies can disagree, every replicated store must make a promise about *how much* they may disagree, as seen from the outside. That promise is called a *consistency model*: a contract between a data store and its clients about which values a read is allowed to return after a write. Vendors tend to bury these contracts in the appendices of their documentation, written in terms only a specialist enjoys. I find it more useful to read them as a menu with prices. Each level of promise costs something, usually latency or availability, and stronger promises cost more:

<svg viewBox="0 0 640 110" style="max-width:640px;width:100%" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-labelledby="consTitle consDesc">
  <title id="consTitle">The consistency spectrum</title>
  <desc id="consDesc">A horizontal gradient bar from stronger consistency (slower, coordination-hungry) to weaker (faster, available), with markers at: linearizable, causal, read-your-writes, eventual.</desc>
  <defs>
    <linearGradient id="cons" x1="0" y1="0" x2="1" y2="0">
      <stop offset="0" stop-color="#d9482b"/>
      <stop offset="1" stop-color="#00c9ff"/>
    </linearGradient>
  </defs>
  <rect x="20" y="40" width="600" height="16" rx="8" fill="url(#cons)"/>
  <text x="20" y="26" font-family="sans-serif" font-size="13" fill="#b23a20" font-weight="bold">stronger — slower, coordination-hungry</text>
  <text x="620" y="26" font-family="sans-serif" font-size="13" fill="#0b7ecb" font-weight="bold" text-anchor="end">weaker — faster, available</text>
  <line x1="70" y1="56" x2="70" y2="72" stroke="#b23a20" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="70" y="90" font-family="sans-serif" font-size="11" fill="#333" text-anchor="middle">linearizable</text>
  <line x1="260" y1="56" x2="260" y2="72" stroke="#b23a20" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="260" y="90" font-family="sans-serif" font-size="11" fill="#333" text-anchor="middle">causal</text>
  <line x1="420" y1="56" x2="420" y2="72" stroke="#0b7ecb" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="420" y="90" font-family="sans-serif" font-size="11" fill="#333" text-anchor="middle">read-your-writes</text>
  <line x1="570" y1="56" x2="570" y2="72" stroke="#0b7ecb" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="570" y="90" font-family="sans-serif" font-size="11" fill="#333" text-anchor="middle">eventual</text>
</svg>

*Figure 4.2 — The consistency spectrum, from linearizable to eventual. Encore buys a different level for each invariant: seat allocation is linearizable, ticket lists are read-your-writes, and view counters are eventual.*

From the strong end to the weak end, the four levels on the menu promise the following, and each is easiest to grasp through an Encore example.

- **Linearizable** is the strongest promise: the store behaves as if there were a single copy of the data, and every operation takes effect at one instant, in one agreed order. Once any write completes, every later read, from anyone, anywhere, sees it. Seat allocation needs this, because two fans must never both be told that seat 14B is theirs.
- **Causal** consistency promises that if one event could have caused another, everyone sees them in that order. Unrelated events may appear in different orders to different readers. Suppose a promoter moves a concert to a larger venue and then publishes an announcement saying so. The announcement was written *because of* the venue change, so no fan should ever read the announcement and then open the concert page to find the old venue still listed. Two unrelated updates, say to two different tours, may appear in either order.
- **Read-your-writes** promises only that *you* see your own changes. Other people may see them later, but the fan who just bought tickets will always find them in "my tickets." This is exactly the guarantee that fixes the empty-list problem above, and it is much cheaper than linearizability, since the store need only track what each client has written, not coordinate every copy on every write.
- **Eventual** consistency promises only that, if writes stop, all copies will sooner or later agree. In the meantime, any read may return an older value. A concert page's view counter is fine with this: if it says 41,200 when the true figure is 41,350, nobody is harmed, and the number will catch up.

The figure's caption states the lesson that matters most. Encore does not choose one consistency level for the whole company. It chooses a level for each *invariant*, each rule the business needs to hold true, and pays only for the strength that rule actually requires.

CAP is the most famous result about these trade-offs, and also the most misquoted. The name stands for consistency, availability, and partition tolerance. It began as Eric Brewer's turn-of-the-millennium conjecture and was proven as a theorem by Seth Gilbert and Nancy Lynch in 2002. What it actually says is narrow and precise. Suppose the network partitions, so that some machines cannot reach others. A request arrives at a machine on one side of the break. That machine cannot check with the other side, so for this *operation* it has exactly two choices: refuse to answer, which keeps consistency because it never returns a value that might be wrong, or answer with whatever data it has, which keeps availability at the risk of returning a stale value. That is all. It is a far narrower claim than the "pick two of three" slogan it travels under, which suggests a designer chooses two properties once, for a whole system, forever. In reality partitions are not optional (networks break whether we choose it or not), the choice applies only while a partition lasts, and it can be made differently for each operation.

PACELC, from Daniel Abadi, completes the picture by covering the time when nothing is broken. The acronym reads as a sentence: if there is a partition (P), choose between availability (A) and consistency (C); else (E), choose between latency (L) and consistency (C). The second clause is the one architects meet every day. Even on a calm afternoon with a healthy network, a strongly consistent write must wait for several machines to confirm it, and that waiting is latency. Partitions are rare; the latency cost of consistency is paid on every request.

At Encore, the choice is made operation by operation. A seat sale chooses consistency: if Seat Inventory cannot be sure a seat is free, it refuses to sell and the fan's screen shows "high demand, retrying," because selling the same seat twice is far worse than a few seconds' wait. A catalog browse always chooses availability: a page that shows a tour date a minute out of date is much better than an error page on announcement day. Both kinds of operation may touch the same database, so that database must let Encore choose *per operation*, not once for the whole cluster. I would put that requirement on the first line of any storage evaluation. It disqualifies half the candidates before the demos start, which saves everyone a great deal of time.

The strong end of the spectrum rests on *consensus*: a family of protocols in which a group of machines agrees on a value, or on the order of writes, and a write counts as done only once a majority of them have accepted it. Leslie Lamport's Paxos is the classic protocol, famous both for its correctness and for being hard to understand. Raft (Diego Ongaro and John Ousterhout, 2014) solves the same problem and was designed explicitly to be understood. An architect does not need to implement either, but needs three facts about how they behave.

First, consensus clusters have odd sizes, and the arithmetic shows why. A cluster stays available as long as a majority of its machines are up. With three machines, the majority is two, so the cluster survives one failure. With four, the majority is three, so it *still* survives only one failure, while paying for an extra machine and needing one more confirmation on every write. A fourth machine buys nothing; a fifth raises the tolerance to two failures. Second, writes go through an elected leader, so when the leader dies, nothing can be written until the remaining machines notice and elect a new one. That failover pause, however long it lasts, is your write downtime, and it belongs in your availability budget. Third, a majority must confirm every write, so if the machines are spread across regions, each write waits for a cross-region round trip: tens of milliseconds within a continent, far more across an ocean. This is not a tuning problem; it is the speed of light. Spanner-style databases have found clever ways to serve strongly consistent *reads* locally, but no product offers writes that are both fast and globally consistent, and a vendor who implies otherwise deserves careful questions.

Leadership also needs an expiry date. Imagine a leader that stalls: a long garbage-collection pause freezes its process, or its virtual machine is migrated to another host, or a network blip cuts it off for a few seconds. The other machines stop hearing from it, conclude it has died, and elect a successor. Then the old leader wakes up. From its point of view no time has passed, so it goes on *believing* it leads and sends the writes it was in the middle of. If storage accepts them, those late writes silently overwrite the new leader's work, and no error appears anywhere. This is the conductor of Section 4.1 who freezes mid-phrase and resumes convinced the orchestra is still following. Two mechanisms guard against it. **Leases** give leadership a time limit: a leader holds its role only for, say, ten seconds at a time and must renew it before it runs out, so a leader that has been frozen knows on waking that its lease may have lapsed and must stop writing. **Fencing tokens** close the remaining gap, since a frozen process cannot always tell how long it was frozen. Each newly elected leader receives a *term* number higher than any before it, every write carries that number, and storage remembers the highest term it has seen and rejects any write stamped with an older one. Encore's seat shards write with fenced terms, so if a stale leader tries to record a seat sale after its successor has taken over, its writes bounce instead of corrupting the seat map. The internals can stay with the database authors. The architect carries one question into every design review: *what stops yesterday's leader from writing today?*

**Recap.** Replicate to survive machine failure and to serve heavy reads; partition when writes outgrow one machine, choosing a key that spreads load and keeps each operation on one shard. Copies drift apart, so a consistency model states which reads are allowed after a write. Encore buys consistency per invariant, and pays for it in latency every day and in availability during a partition, so the architect pins each invariant to a consistency level before evaluating any storage product. I have watched teams do it the other way round, and let the vendor's marketing choose for them.

**Exercise 4.2.** List four data items in a system you know, for example a balance, a profile picture, a shopping cart, or a counter. For each, assign the weakest consistency level from Figure 4.2 that the business tolerates. Then name the level your current database actually delivers for each item, and mark any item where the two differ.

### 4.3 Scalable Service Architectures

#### A reference architecture for scale {#the-reference-shape}

Systems that serve requests at scale tend to end up looking alike. That is not because their builders copied one another, though many did, but because they faced the same forces: traffic that rises and falls, machines that fail, reads that vastly outnumber writes, and work that cannot all be done at once. Bridges that span similar gaps converge on similar shapes for the same reason. Here is the shape most request-serving systems converge on:

{% include diagrams/fig-4-3.svg %}

*Figure 4.3 — The workhorse topology: clients, a load balancer, stateless instances, a distributed cache, a queue with its workers, and the primary store. Highlighted: stateless instances behind the balancer (any instance serves any request) and the queue (which turns spikes into schedules).*

**Statelessness** is what makes the left half of the figure elastic, that is, able to grow and shrink with demand. An instance is stateless when it keeps nothing in its own memory that a later request will need. Whatever must survive between requests, such as who the fan is and what is in their basket, lives either in the distributed cache or in a signed token the client sends with every request. Because no instance holds anything precious, the load balancer can send any request to any instance. Two consequences follow, and both are the point. Scaling up means simply adding instances, since a new one is as useful as an old one from its first request. And losing an instance costs nothing, since the fan's next request goes to a sibling that can read the same cache or token. When an Encore instance dies mid-on-sale, a fan should notice nothing at all.

**Caching** buys read scale, and it charges for it in staleness and invalidation. A cache keeps a copy of data closer to the reader than its true home, so most reads never touch the primary store. The price is that a copy can be out of date (staleness), and someone must decide when to throw it away (invalidation). A morning newspaper is a cache: printed once, delivered to every doorstep, and stale by afternoon. We accept that bargain for the news, and we would never accept it for a bank balance. The useful lesson is that the bargain is struck item by item, not system-wide. The most common pattern is **cache-aside** with **time-to-live (TTL)** expiry. On a read, the service checks the cache first; on a miss, it reads the primary store and puts a copy in the cache, tagged to expire after a set time. That pattern covers most needs, provided you declare *for each item* how stale is acceptable. For Encore's catalog of concerts and venues, minutes are fine: a fan who sees a tour description a few minutes old has lost nothing. For seat availability, no staleness is acceptable, because a fan shown a free seat that was sold a moment ago will try to buy it and fail. So Encore splits the seat map along that line. It caches the map's geometry (the rows, sections and seat positions, which change almost never) and never caches which seats are free.

**The queue** matters most of the three, and the idea is older than the web: the transaction monitors that ran on mainframes were deferring work to be done later decades ago. The difference between a call and a queued message comes down to *when* the other side must be working. A synchronous call needs the service it calls to be alive and responsive *now*, while the caller waits. A queued message needs that service to be alive only *eventually*: the message waits safely in the queue until a worker picks it up. That difference changes what a spike does to Encore. Suppose ticket issuance (generating each ticket and sending it to the fan) sits behind a queue. When an on-sale floods the system, issuance does not fall over; the extra work simply piles up as a backlog, and the workers drain it over the next few minutes. Nobody notices, because by then every fan has already seen "purchase confirmed." That confirmation came from the one step that truly had to be synchronous, and everything after it could wait its turn.

The kata at the end of this chapter will ask for capacity arithmetic, so let us do it for Encore now, step by step. At the worst on-sale, 2,000 fans/sec want in. Each fan who is admitted drives about 3 seat-map reads and about 1 reservation attempt. If Encore admitted everyone, Seat Inventory would face 2,000 × 3 = 6,000 reads/sec and 2,000 writes/sec, and those writes must be serialized, applied one after another in a single order, because two fans must never both win the same seat. Now compare those demands with what one shard can do. One shard sustains about 10,000 reads/sec, so the reads fit comfortably. But it sustains only about 1,500 ordered reservations/sec, and 2,000 is more than that. So it is the *writes*, not the reads, that size the system.

The obvious remedy is to add shards, and here it does not work. Seats are keyed by `event_id` (Section 4.2), which means every seat for one on-sale lives on one shard. Adding shards gives Encore more places to put *other* events, but it cannot raise the ceiling of the one shard that holds tonight's event. So Encore limits the load at the door instead. The Gate admits at most about 1,200 fans/sec per event, which works out to about 1,200 reservation attempts per second against a ceiling of about 1,500, keeping that shard at no more than 80% of its write capacity. (The reads come to about 3,600/sec, well within the shard's 10,000.) The extra shards (four, for headroom) are there to serve *concurrent* on-sales, so that two big events on the same evening do not compete for one shard. An event too big for even one shard would have to be split by section, and that would cost it the single ordered path that makes seat reservations simple to reason about. Notice what three lines of multiplication have done: the design review no longer argues in adjectives such as "fast" or "scalable," it argues in numbers that anyone in the room can check.

> **Queueing intuition.** Why stop at 80%, and not run the shard at 95%? Queueing theory answers. Its most famous result is Little's law, proved by John Little in 1961: the number of items in a system equals their arrival rate times the time each spends in the system. It tells you how much work is in flight. If, say, reservations arrive at 1,200/sec and each spends 50 ms in the shard, then 1,200 × 0.05 = 60 reservations are in progress at any moment. The second result explains the 80% line. In a simple model with one server and random arrivals, the time a request spends waiting in line grows in proportion to 1/(1 − utilization), where utilization is the fraction of time the server is busy; more precisely, the wait is utilization/(1 − utilization) service times. At 50% load, a request waits about one service time; at 90%, about nine. As load approaches 100%, the wait grows without bound. The curve is gentle at first and then bends sharply upward, and above about 80% every burst of arrivals turns into a long queue, because the server has too little idle time left to catch up. Anyone who drives a motorway near capacity has seen this: one driver touches the brakes, the driver behind brakes harder, and a jam forms from nothing, with no accident anywhere. That knee in the curve is why Section 4.4 makes load shedding mandatory. Refusing the work that would push a shard above the knee is what keeps the work below it flowing.

**Scatter/gather** sends one request out to many shards or services at once (the scatter) and assembles their answers into one response (the gather). Its weakness is that the response cannot be complete until the last answer arrives, so the whole request runs at the speed of the slowest participant. That is Section 4.1's tail arithmetic again: the more participants, the more likely one of them is having a slow moment. Three further patterns, catalogued by Brendan Burns for systems running on Kubernetes, complete the vocabulary, and each is a small helper process deployed alongside a service. A **sidecar** is a per-instance helper that takes over a chore such as TLS or telemetry, so the service itself does not have to implement it. An **ambassador** is a local proxy that stands in for remote services: the service talks to something next door, and the ambassador handles finding, connecting to, and retrying the real service far away. An **adapter** puts a uniform interface over diverse services, for example presenting metrics from many different services in one common format. Chapter 5 packages these helpers into the "service mesh."

The reference shape quietly assumes that clients ask and the server answers. Some of what Encore must do runs the other way: the server must *tell* the client something the client did not ask for at that moment, such as live scores, a fan's position in the queue, or "your seat is gone." For that the shape needs a **push tier**. It consists of gateway instances that each hold many long-lived connections open to clients, using WebSocket or Server-Sent Events, and a publish/subscribe (pub/sub) fan-out behind them that delivers each message to every gateway with an interested client. This tier has a different cost model from the rest of the figure, so it is sized differently. What consumes its capacity is the number of connections held open and the number of messages pushed per second, not the number of requests per second. It also has a failure mode of its own, the reconnect storm. Think of a stadium at the final whistle: everyone heads for the exits at once. In the same way, if a gateway fails or a network blip cuts off a million clients together, those million clients all try to reconnect together, and the push tier must survive that surge, typically by having clients wait a randomized interval before reconnecting so that the wave spreads out.

**Recap.** A stateless tier, a cache, and a queue form the reference architecture for scale, and systems that must push to clients add a connection tier sized by open connections and messages. How stale a cached item may be is declared item by item, never once for the whole system. Queues turn spikes into backlogs that drain later, which is why deciding where the asynchronous boundary falls, what must happen now and what may happen eventually, is an architectural decision.

**Exercise 4.3.** Redraw Figure 4.3 for Encore's purchase flow, from the fan's click through to the issued ticket. On your drawing, mark exactly which arrow is the synchronous "moment of truth," the one call whose result the fan must wait for, and explain in a sentence or two for each remaining step why it can safely go through a queue instead.

### 4.4 Resilience Engineering

#### Anatomy of Encore's first outage {#anatomy-of-a-self-inflicted-outage}

Every architect remembers a first real outage, and Encore's came at its first big on-sale after the extraction of Chapter 2. It is worth following one step at a time, because each step looks harmless. It begins outside Encore: the PSP, which normally answers a charge request in 200 ms, slows to 8 s. Each order request that calls the PSP holds a thread while it waits, and because the waits are now forty times longer, threads are taken faster than they are returned, until the pool is empty. The health checks run on that same pool, so they cannot get a thread either, and they begin to report the instances as unhealthy. The autoscaler sees a fleet in trouble and, trying to help, starts more instances. Each new instance opens *more* connections to the PSP, which was already struggling. Meanwhile fans' apps and browsers give up waiting, and their timeouts fire and *retry*, so every stuck purchase becomes several, tripling the load. At the end of this chain the PSP is still processing payments, slowly but correctly, and Encore is down.

Now read the chain again, because the striking thing about it is that no component failed. The thread pool, the health checks, the autoscaler and the client retries each did exactly what its defaults said, and each default was reasonable on its own. A shared pool saves memory; a health check that fails when an instance cannot serve is honest; adding capacity under load is what autoscalers are for; retrying a request that timed out is good manners. It was their composition that brought the site down. That is the lesson I find most worth keeping: a default is a decision someone else made for a situation they never imagined, and an on-sale with a slow PSP is exactly such a situation. Resilience engineering is the discipline of replacing those inherited decisions with deliberate ones, and of breaking the chain at every link, so that no single slowdown can travel all the way to the fan:

| Link in the chain | The breaker |
|---|---|
| Waiting forever on a slow call | **Timeouts**: set aggressively and budgeted end-to-end, so that the parts of a request add up to what the fan will tolerate (of the fan's 3 s budget, ~800 ms goes to the PSP). A charge that timed out is *unknown*, not failed: the PSP may have taken the money. So retry only with the same idempotency key, and reconcile by asking the PSP's status API what actually happened |
| Retries amplifying load | **Backoff + jitter**: wait longer after each failed attempt, by a randomized amount, so that clients do not retry in lockstep. Retry *budgets*: retries never exceed ~10% of traffic. Retries at one layer only, because three layers retrying three times each is 27× load. Idempotency is a prerequisite, since every retry repeats the operation |
| Slowness spreading between dependencies | **Bulkheads**: a separate pool per dependency, so that when the PSP's pool drains, seat-viewing's does not, and fans can still browse while payments struggle |
| Hammering a dying dependency | **Circuit breaker**: after enough failures, fail fast instead of calling; then probe gently, and recover automatically once probes succeed |
| Load exceeding capacity | **Load shedding & backpressure**: reject excess work early and cheaply, with a clear answer, instead of accepting it and failing slowly; that is the Gate's whole job |

*Figure 4.4 — Each link of the outage chain has a named countermeasure. None is exotic, and one missing countermeasure lets the cascade through.*

The first row deserves a moment more, because it holds the subtlest idea in the table. When Encore's call to the PSP times out, Encore knows only that it stopped waiting; it does not know whether the charge happened. The request may have been lost on the way, or the PSP may have charged the card and the reply been lost on the way back. Treating the timeout as a failure and charging again risks taking a fan's money twice; treating it as a success risks issuing a ticket nobody paid for. The honest status is *unknown*, and the cure is the one Section 4.1 prepared: every charge carries an idempotency key, so a retry with the same key cannot charge twice, and a reconciliation step asks the PSP's status API what became of the original attempt before Encore decides anything.

Several of these names come from older engineering, and the analogies teach something if we follow them far enough. A ship's bulkheads are walls that divide the hull into watertight compartments, so that a breach in one floods only that section and the ship stays afloat. The *Titanic* had bulkheads, but they did not rise high enough: as the bow sank, water spilled over the top of each one into the next, and the compartments failed in sequence. A thread pool shared by order processing and health checks is that kind of bulkhead, a partition that looks like isolation but lets the flood over the top, which is just what happened in Encore's outage. The circuit breaker, a name Michael Nygard brought to software in *Release It!* (2007), borrows from the panel in your home, which cuts the current when a fault draws too much of it, so the wiring does not burn. The software version goes a step further than the household one. A household breaker stays off until a person walks down to the basement and resets it; a software breaker tests the line itself, by letting an occasional request through, and closes again on its own once the danger has passed.

{% include diagrams/fig-4-5.svg %}

*Figure 4.5 — The circuit breaker's three states. Closed: calls flow normally. Open: calls fail immediately without reaching the dependency. Half-open: a few trial calls test whether it has recovered. An open breaker stops threads piling up behind a slow call and lets the dependency recover.*

Walk the breaker through Encore's outage and you can see why it helps twice. Closed, it passes calls to the PSP and counts how many fail or time out. When failures cross a threshold, it opens, and from then on order requests that need the PSP get an immediate, clear answer instead of holding a thread for 8 s (Section 4.5 shows what Encore does with that moment). That protects Encore, because threads stop piling up, and it protects the PSP, because it is no longer being flooded while it tries to recover. After a pause the breaker goes half-open and lets a few trial calls through. If they succeed, it closes and traffic resumes; if they fail, it opens again and waits once more.

Recovery has its own trap, and it is easy to miss because it arrives just when everyone relaxes. When a dependency comes back, every breaker that was open closes, every retry that was queued fires, and every cache that expired during the outage is cold, so every request goes to the source. The returning load arrives all at once, and it can be heavy enough to knock the dependency down again, which opens the breakers, which queues more retries, and the cycle repeats. A system caught in this loop after the original trigger is gone is called *metastable* (the term comes from Nathan Bronson and colleagues, 2021): it is stable while it stays down, and the act of recovering is itself what fails. Electricity grid operators know the pattern well. Reconnecting a whole district at once after a blackout would start every refrigerator and air conditioner at the same moment, and that surge would trip the supply again, so operators restore load in blocks, one area at a time. We break the loop the same way: by ramping traffic back to the dependency gradually rather than all at once, by warming caches before reopening to fans, and by letting half-open breakers admit only a trickle of probes, so that a recovering PSP sees a slowly rising flow instead of a wall.

**Recap.** Cascading outages come not from broken components but from working components following their defaults, each reasonable alone and dangerous together. We break every link of the chain deliberately: timeouts budgeted end-to-end, retries with backoff, jitter and a budget, bulkheads that keep one dependency's slowness to itself, breakers that fail fast, and load shedding at the door. A side effect that timed out, such as a charge, is unknown until reconciled, and a recovering dependency must be brought back gradually, or recovery itself becomes the next outage.

**Exercise 4.4.** For your system's most important synchronous dependency, write down five things: its timeout, and how that fits the end-to-end budget; its retry budget, and at which layer retries happen; whether it has a separate pool (a bulkhead); whether it has a circuit breaker; and what users see when it is down. Every blank is work for your next sprint.

### 4.5 Degradation and Chaos

The patterns of the last section stop a failure from spreading. They do not, by themselves, decide what the fan sees while the failure lasts. That is the job of **graceful degradation**: failure behavior that someone designed beforehand, on a calm day, instead of improvising during the outage. A hospital is the classic example. When it loses mains power, the generators do not try to run everything. They carry the operating theaters and the intensive care ward, while the cafeteria and the gift shop go dark, because someone sat down before the storm and ranked what matters most. The ranking is the design. Without it, the generators would be shared out by whatever happened to be switched on, and the surgeon would compete with the coffee machine.

Encore makes that kind of decision about its payment service provider, the PSP whose slowdown set off the cascade in Section 4.4. The design has three tiers, and it helps to walk through them in order, as a fan would experience them.

In the first tier, nothing is wrong. Orders & Payments integrates two providers, and every payment goes to the primary.

In the second tier, the primary is failing. Its circuit breaker opens, and from that moment *new* payment attempts route to the secondary provider instead. The word "new" matters, and the reason is idempotency (Section 4.1). A payment that was already under way at the primary when the breaker opened stays where it started: it either finishes there or fails there. Encore does not resend it to the secondary, because the primary may have charged the card without managing to say so, and sending the same payment to a second provider would risk charging the fan twice. Idempotency keys are therefore scoped to each provider: a retry goes back, under the same key, to the provider that saw the first attempt, and that provider can recognize it as a repeat. To the fan, the switch is invisible; the payment page simply works.

In the third tier, both providers are degraded at once, and there is nowhere left to send a payment. Only then does Encore fall back to a **pending payment**. Normally a fan's seats are held for five minutes while they pay. Under a pending payment, a verified fan's seats stay held for up to twenty minutes instead. Encore tells the fan plainly what is happening, and as soon as a provider recovers, it sends them a link to complete the payment, including strong customer authentication. Through all three tiers, one rule never bends: no ticket is ever issued without an authorized payment. The pending hold keeps the fan's place; it does not give them the ticket.

None of this is free, and I want to be clear that the costs are part of the design, not a footnote to it. There are three of them. First, a second provider is a second integration that someone must build, test, and reconcile against Encore's own records, whether or not it is ever called on. Second, a pending hold takes seats off sale for people who may never come back to pay. During an on-sale, when thousands of fans are waiting for those very seats, twenty minutes is a long time. Third, an unpaid hold is a gift to bots: if holding seats costs nothing, a scalper's script can grab hundreds and decide later which to keep.

Encore bounds each of these costs on purpose. Pending holds are offered only to verified fans, which keeps anonymous bots out. They are capped per fan, so no single account can sweep up a section. And a hold that is never paid lapses through the saga compensation of Chapter 5, which undoes the reservation step by step and puts the seats back on sale. The fallback is generous to real fans, but it is fenced in.

There is a tempting shortcut that would seem to make all of this unnecessary: accept the order now, issue the ticket, and charge the card later, once a provider is back. Encore cannot take it, for two independent reasons. The first is regulatory. Europe's revised Payment Services Directive (PSD2) requires strong customer authentication, which means the fan must confirm a card payment, typically in their banking app, at the moment the payment is made. A charge that runs later, with no fan present to authenticate it, is exactly what the rule forbids. The second reason is architectural. Encore never stores card numbers (Chapter 10); the PSP holds them. So even if the law allowed a later charge, Encore would have nothing to charge. The shortcut is closed twice over, which is why the design keeps the seats and the fan's place, and leaves the payment itself until the fan can make it.

What does the fan actually experience? Sales carry on. The message on the screen says, in effect, "your seats are held; we'll send you a link to pay," and once a provider recovers, the link arrives. The outage is soon forgotten, and that is the highest compliment a degraded mode can receive: nobody remembers it.

A design like this is only a promise until we have seen it work, and that is where **chaos testing** comes in. Chaos testing means deliberately injecting failures into a running system to check that it behaves the way its design claims. In the language of Chapter 1, it turns each resilience claim into a fitness function: an automated, repeatable check that the architecture still has a property we care about. Netflix made the practice famous in the early 2010s with Chaos Monkey, a tool that randomly shut down its own production servers so that engineers had to build services that survived the loss. The spirit is much older than that. A fire drill is a chaos experiment: it tests the stairwells, the alarms, and the people on a calm day, so that nobody discovers on the day of a real fire that an exit is locked.

For Encore, a chaos experiment looks like this. In staging, we kill an instance of a service, and we inject 5 s of latency into calls to the PSP. Then we assert three things: that the circuit breakers open, that new payments move to the second provider, and that the queues of waiting work drain instead of piling up. If any assertion fails, we have found a gap in the design on our own terms, rather than in front of thousands of fans during an on-sale. My conviction here is simple: a resilience pattern you have never watched fire is a guess.

**Recap.** Graceful degradation is failure behavior designed in advance. Encore's version has three tiers: the primary PSP, failover of new payments to a second PSP, and, only when both are degraded, capped pending-payment holds for verified fans. The costs of that design (a second integration, seats held for people who may not pay, and an opening for bots) are bounded on purpose, and no ticket is issued without an authorized payment. Chaos experiments then turn resilience from a claim into a tested property.

**Exercise 4.5.** Pick one dependency your product cannot work without. Write down three things: the degraded behavior users would see during a one-hour outage of that dependency, what that behavior costs the business (in money, risk, or abuse), and the chaos experiment that would prove the degraded mode works, including what you would inject and what you would assert.

### Kata: Beacon

A kata, in the martial arts, is a set form practiced until the moves come without thought. Ours are design problems, and this one uses every tool the chapter has handed us. Beacon is not Encore, but the two are cousins. Encore's hardest moments are on-sales, when a crowd arrives at once and leaves when the seats are gone. Beacon's are match days, when a crowd arrives at once and stays for ninety minutes, waiting for something to happen. Beacon also serves two audiences who want very different things from the same event. Its central lesson is that one system can make two kinds of promise, provided it does not try to keep both with the same machinery.

> **Your brief: "Beacon."** Live-score platform for a football league. 2 million concurrent fans on match days (35 million peak during cup finals), each expecting score updates within 2 s of the referee's whistle. Near-zero traffic between matches. A betting-partner API has a *hard* requirement: odds-relevant events (goals, red cards) must be delivered in order, with exactly-once effect, with p99 < 500 ms. Fifteen engineers. Cloud budget: "startup, not sovereign wealth fund."
>
> *Hint:* No network can promise that a message is *delivered* exactly once, because a lost acknowledgment looks the same as a lost message, so the sender must send again. "Exactly-once effect" is therefore achieved as at-least-once delivery plus idempotent consumers: the betting partner may receive the same goal twice, but it applies that goal only once. Section 6.3 treats it in full. The fan-facing tier is a push tier (Section 4.3): Beacon holds open connections to fans and tells them about each goal, instead of waiting for them to ask.

Before you start, read the brief once more for its tensions, because that is where the design lives. Two million fans on an ordinary match day, rising to 35 million for a cup final, is a swing of more than seventeen times. Between matches the traffic falls to almost nothing, so a fleet sized for the final would sit idle most of the week, at a cost the budget line forbids. The fans want speed but will forgive a score that arrives a moment late or, now and then, not at all until the next refresh. The betting partner forgives nothing: a goal delivered out of order, or counted twice, moves real money on the wrong odds. And fifteen engineers cannot run a cathedral. Whatever you build, they must be able to operate it, pager in hand, on a Saturday night.

**Deliverables:**

1. **Capacity model** — a table of reads/sec and writes/sec at match peak and at final peak, the fan-out factor per score update (how many messages one goal turns into once it is pushed to every connected fan), and the arithmetic that sizes the fan-facing tier. Show the multiplication, as Section 4.3 did for Encore's on-sale.
2. **Topology** — a diagram like Figure 4.3, marking the synchronous moment(s) of truth (the steps that must complete before anyone can be told "it happened"), every queue, and every cache with its declared staleness.
3. **Consistency ledger** — a side-by-side comparison of fan scores and betting events, giving for each its consistency level, its ordering strategy, and its idempotency mechanism.
4. **Failure-mode analysis** — for three failures (instance death, a stall in the stats provider that feeds you match events, and the loss of a whole region): the behavior you have designed for each, and the chaos experiment that proves it.
5. **Two architecture decision records (ADRs)** — one-page records in Chapter 1's format, each capturing a single decision with its context and consequences: one for the load-shedding policy for final-day overload, and one for the betting-API delivery design.

**Rubric:**

| Criterion | The question to ask yourself |
|---|---|
| Arithmetic | Does the capacity model survive multiplication, or is it guesswork dressed up in units? |
| Tail budgets | Are latency promises stated at p99, and is the budget divided hop by hop so that the parts add up to the whole? |
| Two-speed design | Are fans (eventual, cheap) and bookmakers (ordered, exact) served by *different* machinery? |
| Failure authorship | Is behavior under failure designed and chaos-tested, or inherited from defaults? |
| Overload policy | Does the load-shedding ADR say which traffic is shed first on final day, and what those fans see? |

A word on the third row, since it is the one most designs miss. The tempting move is to build a single pipeline good enough for the bookmakers and let the fans ride along on it. But ordered delivery with exactly-once effect at p99 < 500 ms is expensive per message, and multiplying that cost by millions of fans would spend the whole budget on a guarantee the fans never asked for. The opposite shortcut fails as well: a cheap broadcast that may drop or reorder messages is fine for a fan's phone and unacceptable for a betting partner. The good answers split the stream early. A small, strict, ordered path serves the few partners who need exactness, and a wide, cheap, forgiving path serves the many who need only speed. Both are fed by the same referee's whistle.

#### Where you now stand

Look back at what this chapter has given you. You know the false assumptions the network tempts us to make, and what each one costs when we make it anyway. You know the price list of consistency, and how to choose an item from it per piece of data. You have the reference topology of stateless instances, caches and queues, with a push tier added when the server must tell instead of being asked. And you have the resilience chain of timeouts, retry budgets, bulkheads, breakers and load shedding, along with degraded modes designed in advance and the chaos experiments that prove they work. All of that concerns a single service, or a small handful of them. What remains is running a *fleet* of such services, each one modeled, contracted, deployed, observed, and owned by a team that answers the pager when it breaks. That is the subject of Chapter 5.
