## Chapter 11: Cloud-Native Operations — Serverless, Kubernetes, and SRE {#ch11}

> Architecture that cannot be operated is fiction with diagrams.


Every chapter so far has stopped at a convenient place: the moment the design is finished and, for someone else, the real work begins. We drew the boundaries, chose the patterns and argued the trade-offs, and then we turned the page. But the system we designed must now actually run. It must run at 3 a.m., when the only person awake is whoever holds the pager. It must run in the middle of an on-sale, when a crowd of fans is pressing "buy" for the same seats in the same minute. And it must run within a budget that someone has to sign. In this chapter that someone is you. This is why I don't think of operations as what happens *after* architecture. Operations is architecture's feedback loop. Every trade-off we drew as a tidy table in an earlier chapter eventually comes back to us in one of two forms: as an invoice at the end of the month, or as a page in the middle of the night. A design that looked elegant on the whiteboard but is expensive to run, or hard to diagnose when it fails, was never as good as it looked; operations is where we find that out.

The chapter is organized around three questions, each of which an operating team faces every day. First, where should each workload run: on virtual machines, in containers, as serverless functions, or inside a managed service that someone else operates? Second, how does a change travel from an engineer's laptop to production without breaking what fans are using? Third, how do we know the system is working, and how reliable does it need to be? That last question brings in site reliability engineering (SRE), the discipline of treating reliability as something we set targets for and measure, and it brings in cost, because in the cloud the price of a decision arrives every month. Encore supplies the examples throughout, including one bill that nobody enjoyed reading, and the arithmetic behind it.

My own conviction here comes from practice. I spent years as a platform tech lead in a large bank, moving a platform with over a million users toward the cloud. The migrations that worked were never lift-and-shift, the approach of taking an application exactly as it runs on premises and simply moving it onto rented cloud machines. Lift-and-shift changes where the system runs without changing what it can do, so it tends to inherit all the old problems and add a new bill on top. The migrations that succeeded were different: each cloud version had to solve a problem that the on-premises version could not, or it had no reason to exist. That gave us one question to ask of every workload: what does the new runtime actually fix? Was it faster scaling for a spiky load, less patching for a small team, or recovery from a data-center failure? Asking that question told us more than any capacity spreadsheet, because a spreadsheet tells you how much machinery you need, but not whether moving it is worth doing at all.

### 11.1 Cloud Execution Models

#### Execution models and what you still operate {#the-spectrum-priced-in-responsibility}

The first operational question is where each workload should run, and the cloud offers a whole menu of answers: virtual machines, containers, functions, fully managed services. It is tempting to compare them on speed or price, but I find it more useful to compare them in a different currency: how much undifferentiated machinery you still own once you have chosen. By *undifferentiated* I mean work that must be done well for the system to run, yet that no customer will ever choose Encore for doing well. Patching an operating system is the classic example, and so is upgrading a cluster. A fan buying tickets to a stadium show does not care whether Encore's kernels are current; they care only that the seat map loads and the payment goes through. Every hour Encore's engineers spend on such work is an hour not spent on what makes Encore worth choosing, so the execution models in Figure 11.1 are best read as a ladder of how much of that work each one takes off your hands.

| Model | You own | You've shed | Fits |
|---|---|---|---|
| Virtual machines (VMs) | the operating system (OS) and everything above it | hardware | legacy software, special kernels |
| Containers on Kubernetes | container images, deployment manifests, cluster policy | machines, scheduling | steady services, rich ecosystems |
| Serverless containers, autopilot Kubernetes (Cloud Run, Fargate, GKE Autopilot, EKS Auto Mode) | container images, service configuration | nodes, cluster upgrades, most scaling | container workloads without a platform team |
| Serverless functions | code and configuration | servers, scaling, idle cost | spiky, event-shaped work |
| Managed services (queues, databases, gateways) | configuration | the entire service | everything that isn't your differentiator |

*Figure 11.1 — The responsibility spectrum, from models where you own the most (top) to those where you own the least (bottom). Read the default bottom-up: managed services first, functions for spiky work, containers for steady work (on a managed runtime before a self-run cluster), and VMs only when nothing else fits. Differentiation breaks ties, as in Chapter 3's subdomain table: core subdomains get your operational attention, generic ones a managed service.*

Read from top to bottom, the table is a story of shedding. On a VM, the provider owns the hardware and you own everything from the operating system up. Each row below hands one more layer to the provider, until at the bottom you own nothing but configuration. The figure's advice is to start at the bottom and climb only as far as a workload forces you to. Take a managed service if one fits; reach for functions if the work arrives in bursts; use containers for steady work, preferably on a runtime someone else operates; fall back to VMs only for software that cannot live anywhere else, such as a legacy product that needs a particular kernel. When two rows seem equally good, the tiebreaker is the one Chapter 3 gave us for subdomains. Where Encore competes, in its core subdomains, the extra control of a higher row may be worth owning. Where the work is generic, a managed service wins, because owning generic machinery buys nothing a customer will notice.

The table also hides an old idea, older than the cloud by half a century. In 1961 John McCarthy suggested that computing might one day be organized as a public utility, sold by the unit like water or electricity. The time-sharing systems of that decade were the first step toward it. MIT's Compatible Time-Sharing System let many users share one expensive machine, each working as though the machine were, more or less, their own. Multics later set out to be exactly such a computer utility. What those systems began, the bottom rows of Figure 11.1 have made ordinary: a managed queue or a serverless function is computing drawn from a tap and paid for by the unit.

The utility analogy is worth taking further, because it teaches something real and then breaks in an instructive place. Consider a city's water supply. Nobody digs a well in the back garden when the main is reliable, and in the same way nobody at Encore should run their own message broker when a managed queue will do the job. But the analogy breaks because water is the same from every tap, whereas managed services are not interchangeable. Each one has its own limits, its own ways of failing and its own pricing quirks. A managed queue might cap message size, throttle a burst of publishes, or charge per request in a way that makes a chatty design expensive. Those details become part of your architecture whether or not you read the fine print, so the utility frees you from operating the machinery but not from understanding it.

Kubernetes occupies the second row of the table, and it repays a closer look, because its central idea is simpler than its reputation. Kubernetes is best understood as a *declarative reconciliation engine*. "Declarative" means you describe the state you want rather than the steps to reach it: "run six copies of the Catalog service, each with this much memory." "Reconciliation" means a control loop that keeps comparing that desired state with the actual state and acts to close the gap. A thermostat works the same way. You set the room to 21°C and never tell it when to switch the heating on; it simply notices the difference between what is and what should be, and corrects it, over and over. So when a node dies and takes two Catalog copies with it, nobody at Encore has to restart them. The loop sees four copies where six were declared and starts two more elsewhere.

That loop, more than any particular YAML file, is why Kubernetes won. Kubernetes descends from Borg, Google's internal cluster manager, and it inherited Borg's habit of treating the cluster as one machine to be steered toward a declared goal. Its most valued features all follow from that one loop. Self-healing is the loop replacing what has failed. Bin-packing is the scheduler placing workloads onto machines so that their capacity is used efficiently, the way one packs boxes into a van. The large ecosystem of extensions exists because anyone can write a new kind of desired state and a new loop to reconcile it, so databases, certificates and message brokers can all be managed in the same style.

The catch is the price. Running Kubernetes yourself means owning a platform's worth of complexity: cluster upgrades, networking, access control, the extensions themselves, and the on-call duty for all of it. For most product teams that complexity is accidental, in the sense of Brooks's distinction from Chapter 1. It arises from the tools we chose rather than from the problem we are solving, and it is no part of selling tickets. A team that runs its own Kubernetes without a platform team behind it has, in effect, taken up a hobby that comes with an on-call rotation. The managed middle row of Figure 11.1 (Cloud Run, Fargate, GKE Autopilot, EKS Auto Mode) removes most of that work, because the provider runs the nodes, applies the upgrades and handles most of the scaling, while you keep the familiar container model. That is why it is the sensible starting point for most teams in 2026.

Serverless functions change the economics in a different way: they invert the usual cost curve. With servers or containers, you pay for capacity whether or not anyone uses it, so a quiet hour costs as much as a busy one. With functions, you pay per invocation, nothing runs while nothing is asked of it, and the platform scales out quickly when requests arrive. Zero idle spend, fast scale-out and per-invocation billing are the headline. The fine print matters just as much. A *cold start* is the delay when the platform must create a fresh instance of your function before it can serve a request, and it lands on whichever user happened to trigger it. Functions also have execution time limits, so long jobs do not fit. They are stateless, so anything that must survive between calls has to live in an external store. And their scaling is fast but finite: AWS Lambda, for example, adds about 1,000 concurrent executions per function every 10 seconds, within an account-wide quota. A spike that grows faster than that ramp will queue or be throttled, however elastic the brochure sounds.

The architect's job, then, is to match each cost curve to the shape of a workload's traffic, and Encore offers two clear contrasts. The Catalog, which serves event listings, search and event details, is steady and latency-sensitive. Fans browse it all day, the load rises and falls gently, and every page view expects a quick answer. Capacity that is always on is therefore always used, and cold starts would only add delay, so the Catalog runs in containers.

The Sale Gate has the opposite shape. Between on-sales it sits almost idle, and then, when a popular tour goes on sale, it takes 500× its normal load for a few minutes. Paying around the clock for capacity sized to that spike would mean paying for idle machines nearly all the time, so its stateless admission edge, the part that receives each fan and decides when they may enter, suits serverless functions. The state that must survive, each fan's queue position, is held in a managed store rather than inside the functions. Serverless does not make the problem disappear, though. The spike's ramp must still fit within the scaling limits we just met, and cold starts arrive at the worst possible moment, the very second the sale opens. So before each scheduled on-sale Encore pre-warms capacity, using provisioned concurrency to keep a set number of function instances initialized and ready. That is idle spend again, which is a small irony, but it is bounded: Encore pays for warm capacity only during the minutes around a known on-sale. It still costs far less than keeping the spike's capacity running in always-on containers through every quiet day of the year.

**Recap.** We own as little machinery as we can: managed services for generic work, functions for spiky work, containers for steady work, and VMs only when nothing else fits. Kubernetes is a reconciliation model, a loop that keeps reality matching a declared state, and its complexity pays for itself only when a platform team owns it; for everyone else, the managed middle row is the place to start. Serverless functions offer a cost curve with no idle spend, but they come with scaling limits and cold starts that must be planned for. The choice is made per workload, by the shape of its traffic, as Encore's steady Catalog and spiky Sale Gate show.

**Exercise 11.1.** Take three workloads you know. For each, write down its traffic shape (steady, spiky or scheduled), its latency tolerance and the Figure 11.1 row it should occupy. Then compare that row with where the workload actually runs today. For any mismatch, record it and estimate its monthly cost, either in idle capacity you pay for but do not use or in the operations effort your team spends running machinery it could have shed.

### 11.2 Delivery Infrastructure

#### Infrastructure as code and GitOps {#the-environment-is-code-and-git-is-the-control-plane}

Before we can talk about how change reaches production safely, we need production itself to be something we can describe precisely, and that is the job of the first discipline, **infrastructure as code** (IaC). Under IaC, every cluster, queue and permission that Encore runs is declared in versioned text files rather than created by hand. Because the declaration is text under version control, it earns three properties that hand-built infrastructure never has. It can be reviewed before it changes, like any other pull request. It can be diffed while it changes, so we see exactly which queue gains a partition or which role gains a permission. And it can be rebuilt after a disaster, since the files describe the whole environment and not merely the parts someone remembers. The rule that follows is simple to state: the cloud console is for looking, never for changing.

That rule sounds pedantic until we follow one console change to its end. Suppose the Orders queue backs up during an on-sale at 2 a.m., and the engineer on call raises its retention limit by hand in the console. The fix works and the night is saved, but nobody records it, and the code still declares the old limit. Encore now has a **snowflake environment**: one whose true configuration exists only in the running system and nowhere in the files that claim to describe it. Like a real snowflake, it is unique and cannot be reproduced. The next time the environment is rebuilt from code, whether for a disaster drill or a new region, the old limit quietly returns, and the problem the engineer fixed at 2 a.m. comes back weeks later with nobody remembering why it ever went away. That is how a single unrecorded fix causes trouble for the rest of the quarter.

An old building shows the same failure. A building whose wiring no longer matches its drawings is perfectly safe as long as nobody consults the drawings. The danger arrives the day an electrician trusts them and cuts a wire marked dead that is in fact live. An environment that no longer matches its code fails in exactly this way: it runs well until someone acts on what the code says, by rebuilding, scaling or auditing it, and the code turns out to be wrong.

The second discipline, **GitOps**, closes the loop that IaC leaves open. IaC tells us what the environment should be, but nothing yet forces the environment to stay that way. GitOps adds agents that run continuously inside each cluster, compare what is running with what the repository declares, and correct any difference they find. This is the reconciliation idea from Section 11.1 applied one level up. Kubernetes reconciles running containers to the manifests it has been given; a GitOps agent reconciles the manifests themselves to the repository. If the engineer at 2 a.m. had changed the queue by hand in a GitOps world, the agent would have reported the drift, or simply put the declared value back, and the change would have had to go through git to stick.

Because every change now flows through the repository, git becomes three things at once. It is the deployment mechanism, since merging a change is how the change reaches production. It is the audit log, since every commit records who changed what, when and with whose review. And it is the rollback for configuration and stateless services, since an ordinary `git revert` restores the previous declaration and the agents converge the cluster back to it. Rolling back Encore's Catalog after a bad release becomes one reviewed commit rather than a sequence of remembered commands.

There is one important exception, and it is data. A `git revert` restores files, but it cannot restore the rows a schema migration has already rewritten, or bring back a column that has been dropped. So schema migrations and data changes do not rely on revert at all. They ship as expand–contract steps, the pattern Chapter 5 used for evolving service contracts, here applied to the database, and each step is safe to leave in place. First we expand: add the new column or table alongside the old, so that both the old code and the new code work against the same database. Only once nothing reads the old shape do we contract and remove it. If the release that uses the new column must be rolled back, the expanded schema simply stays, harmless, because the old code never needed it to go away. With that exception handled, drift stops being folklore passed between engineers and becomes a diff anyone can read.

On this base, Chapter 5's progressive delivery can run without anyone watching it by hand. A **canary** release sends a small share of real traffic to the new version while the rest stays on the old one, the baseline. The canary is promoted, step by step, only when its **service level indicators (SLIs)**, the measurements of what users actually experience, such as the share of seat-map requests that succeed quickly, match the baseline's. If the canary's error rate or latency diverges, the rollout stops and reverses on its own. How much risk the rollouts may take at all is set by Section 11.3's error budget: while users have been well served, Encore can afford bolder rollouts, and when the budget runs low, rollouts slow down or stop.

**Feature flags** add a second separation, between deploying code and releasing behavior. Deploying puts new code on the servers; releasing lets fans see what it does. With a flag, Encore can deploy a redesigned checkout weeks before an on-sale with the new behavior switched off, and then turn it on for staff, then for a few percent of fans, then for everyone, all without another deployment. If the new checkout misbehaves, switching the flag off takes seconds, which is far faster than any rollback.

**Preview environments** solve the last problem: where to try a change before it merges. Each pull request gets its own short-lived copy of the relevant services, built from the same IaC files as production and torn down when the pull request closes. Two old frustrations end together. "It works on my machine" loses its force, because the change is now tested in an environment built the same way as production rather than on one engineer's laptop. And the queue for shared staging disappears, because nobody waits for another team to finish with the single staging environment before trying their own change.

Once all of this is in place we need a way to tell whether it is working, and the **DevOps Research and Assessment (DORA) metrics** provide that score. The book *Accelerate* introduced four of them. Deployment frequency is how often a team ships to production. Lead time is how long a change takes to travel from commit to production. Change-failure rate is the share of deployments that cause a failure in production. Time-to-restore is how long it takes to recover once such a failure happens. DORA has since renamed the last of these *failed deployment recovery time* and added a fifth, *deployment rework rate*, the share of deployments that are unplanned, made to fix a problem that reached users. It now groups the five into two families: throughput, which asks how fast change flows, and instability, which asks how often change goes wrong.

These metrics measure the delivery system as a whole, not the virtue of any one team, and the distinction matters. A team's lead time depends on its pipeline, its review process, its tests and the coupling of its code to other teams' code, and most of those are matters of design rather than effort. Using the metrics to rank teams against each other would mostly reward teams for the systems they happened to inherit, and would tempt everyone to game the numbers. Read as a description of the system, though, they are valuable, and DORA's research links them to organizational performance.

Encore's dashboard shows the metrics per service, and the team reads a creeping lead time as feedback about the architecture rather than about the people. Suppose a change to the Catalog that once shipped in a day now takes a week. The engineers have rarely grown slower in the meantime. More often, coupling has crept back: the Catalog change now needs a matching change in another service, and the two must be coordinated and released together. Or the tests have slowed, so that every change waits behind a long suite before it can merge. Either way, the cause lies in the structure of the system, and the lead time is simply where that structure first becomes visible. Operational symptoms are often architectural causes seen from a distance.

**Recap.** IaC makes environments reproducible by declaring them in reviewed, versioned files, and GitOps keeps the running system matched to those files, which makes git the control plane and `git revert` the rollback for everything except data; data changes ship as expand–contract steps instead. On that base, canaries are promoted only when they match their baseline and the error budget allows, feature flags separate deploying from releasing, and preview environments replace the queue for shared staging. DORA metrics score the delivery system as a whole, and when they worsen they often flag an architectural problem before anyone has named it.

**Exercise 11.2.** Measure your DORA metrics for last month, however roughly: write down a number for each of deployment frequency, lead time, change-failure rate and time-to-restore (and deployment rework rate if you can). Which is worst, and is its cause operational (pipeline, approvals) or architectural (coupling, test depth)? Give one concrete piece of evidence for your answer, such as a change that waited on another team or a test suite that delays every merge.

### 11.3 Observability and SRE

#### Observability signals, SLOs, and incidents {#from-is-it-up-to-why-is-it-weird}

The third question of this chapter is how we know the system works, and the answer begins with instruments we have already fitted. Chapter 5 installed them: logs that record what happened, metrics that count it, traces that follow one request across services, and correlation IDs that tie the three together. What has changed since then is how those signals leave a service. In 2026 the default is **OpenTelemetry**, a vendor-neutral standard for emitting telemetry. Encore's services instrument their code once, against OpenTelemetry's interfaces, and the choice of where the data goes (one monitoring vendor, another, or an in-house store) becomes configuration rather than code. This is Parnas's information hiding from Chapter 1, applied to telemetry. The monitoring backend is exactly the kind of decision likely to change, because contracts expire and prices rise, so the instrumentation hides it, and switching backends no longer means touching every service.

Signals alone, though, decide nothing. A dashboard full of graphs tells us what the system is doing, not whether that is good enough. Site reliability engineering, the practice Google began building in 2003, supplies the missing step: a short chain of definitions that turns measurements into decisions. The first link is the **service level indicator (SLI)**, a measurement of what users actually experience, written as a ratio of good events to all events. For Encore, one SLI is "the share of seat-map requests served in ≤ 800 ms." A fan neither knows nor cares about CPU or queue depth; they know whether the seat map appeared promptly, and the SLI counts exactly that. The second link is the **service level objective (SLO)**, the promise made about that ratio over a stated window: "99.9% over a rolling 28 days." The third link is the **error budget**, the part of the promise left over, here 0.1%. In request terms that is about 1 slow or failed seat-map request in every 1,000. In time terms it is roughly 40 minutes per 28 days of full-outage equivalent, meaning that if every failure arrived as a complete outage, the budget would run out after about 40 minutes of it.

The budget is the clever part, and it helps to remember the problem it solves. Product teams want to ship features quickly; operations teams want the system left alone so it stays up. Both are right, and for decades the argument between them was settled by whoever was more senior or more persistent in the meeting. The error budget replaces that argument with a policy agreed in advance. While budget remains, the team ships at full speed, because users are getting the reliability they were promised. When the budget is spent, the policy shifts the team's effort to stability until the indicator recovers. Neither side has to win a debate, because both agreed to the rule before anything went wrong:

{% include diagrams/fig-11-2.svg %}

*Figure 11.2 — The error budget as automatic policy. The remaining budget, not a meeting, decides whether the team keeps shipping or turns to stabilizing, so product and reliability turn one shared dial instead of arguing.*

What makes an error budget useful is that it admits imperfection openly. Consider the alternative. A target of 100% sounds admirable, but every change carries some risk of failure, so a 100% target logically forbids every deploy, and in practice it simply gets ignored. A target of 99.9% is honest: it says that users can tolerate one bad request in a thousand, and it grants the team that measured allowance of failure to spend on progress. Each new feature, migration or experiment draws on the allowance, and when the allowance runs low, the team knows it is time to slow down.

Alerting follows the same logic of starting from the user. We page an engineer on *symptoms*, evidence that users are being hurt right now, and only rarely on *causes*, conditions inside the system that might or might not hurt anyone. The usual symptom alert is an SLO **burn rate**: how fast the error budget is being consumed compared with the steady pace that would spend it exactly over the 28 days. A burn rate of 1 is fine; a burn rate of 10 means the budget will be gone in under three days, and fans are already seeing slow seat maps. High CPU, by contrast, is a cause. It may mean trouble, or it may mean the machine is doing its job efficiently during an on-sale, so it does not justify waking anyone. The exceptions are causes that make harm both imminent and certain, even though users feel nothing yet: a disk that will fill within hours, a certificate about to expire, a backup that failed. Those page, because waiting for the symptom would mean waiting for the outage. Everything else goes on a dashboard to be read during business hours.

The reason for this discipline is human as much as technical. A smoke detector that sounds every time someone uses the oven is soon unplugged, and then it is silent on the night of a real fire. Pagers work the same way: an alert that fires without users being affected trains engineers to ignore alerts. An on-call rotation paged only when users are affected, with a runbook (a written, step-by-step response) for each alert, is sustainable for years. A rotation paged on causes wears people out, and the cruel part is who it wears out first: the engineers who instrumented the most, because they created the most alerts. Diligence should not be punished with sleepless nights.

Even with good alerting, things will break, and what we do afterward decides whether the same thing breaks again. The practice is the **blameless postmortem**: a written review of an incident that asks how the system allowed the failure, not who caused it. Aviation learned long ago that punishing pilots for errors does not make errors rarer; it makes them hidden, because people stop reporting what they would be punished for. Software has borrowed the lesson. A blameless postmortem gets the honest account of what happened, and that account is what lets the fix go into the architecture rather than into a reminder to be careful. Encore's retry-storm outage (Chapter 4) is the example. Its postmortem did not end with "engineers should configure retries more carefully." It produced retry budgets in the service mesh defaults, so every service inherits them, and a fitness function in the Chapter 1 style, an automated check that guards the fix. The next storm is prevented by the system itself, without asking anyone to remember anything. An incident is tuition the system has already paid, and the postmortem is how we collect the education.

Incident response is also where AI assistants have become genuinely useful. They now correlate related alerts into one incident, summarize long traces, draft the incident timeline and suggest the runbook step that matches what they see. Those are all reading tasks, and an assistant is good at reading quickly at 3 a.m. Acting is another matter. We should treat these assistants as Chapter 12 will treat any agent: read access by default, and a human approving any action that changes production, so that a confident but wrong suggestion stays a suggestion.

**Recap.** SLIs measure what users experience, SLOs set the target for that measurement, and the error budget, the failure the target allows, turns the old argument between speed and stability into a policy agreed in advance. We page on symptoms such as a fast budget burn, each page backed by a runbook, and keep most causes on dashboards, paging on a cause only when harm is imminent and certain. Postmortems are blameless so that people tell the truth, and they are worth holding only if they end in architectural changes rather than appeals to be careful.

**Exercise 11.3.** Pick your product's most user-visible operation. Write its SLI as a ratio of good events to all events, its SLO as a target over a window, and its error budget both as a share of requests and as minutes of full outage per window. Then go through last month's alerts and sort each one: did it fire on a symptom (users affected) or on a cause (something internal that might affect them)? Count each group.

### 11.4 Cost, Capacity, and Sustainability

#### Cost as telemetry {#the-invoice-is-a-telemetry-stream}

In a data center of our own, most of the cost of an architectural decision was paid once, up front, when the servers were bought; after that, a wasteful design merely used up hardware we already owned. The cloud changes the accounting. Every architectural decision is now billed monthly, for as long as it stays in production: an extra service, an extra copy of the data, an extra hop between regions all show up on the next invoice. That makes the bill an operational signal, read alongside latency and error rates, and not merely a figure for the accountants to reconcile after the fact. FinOps, the practice of managing cloud spend as a shared engineering and financial discipline, gives architects three moves for reading that signal and acting on it.

**Unit economics.** The first move is to stop looking at the total. Raw spend on its own is noise, because it rises with success as readily as with waste: if Encore's bill doubles in the month of a stadium tour, that may simply mean it sold twice as many tickets. What carries signal is spend divided by a unit of business, so Encore tracks *cost per ticket sold*. That number should stay roughly flat as sales grow. When it drifts from €0.11 to €0.19, the business has not changed, so something architectural has: a chatty new service calling another on every request, a query that lost its index and now scans a table, or logging that has quietly grown verbose. The graph shows the drift before the chief financial officer (CFO) asks about it, and it points engineers toward the change that caused it.

Two line items on the bill deserve standing suspicion, because they grow out of design choices rather than out of traffic. The first is egress, the charge for data leaving a provider's network or crossing between its regions. It is priced per byte, so a design in which services in different regions keep calling each other pays again every time it moves its own data back and forth. The second is the volume of logs and traces. It is often one of the larger items on the bill, because every request can produce many lines of telemetry, and each line is paid for when it is ingested and again for every day it is stored. Deciding what fraction of traces to keep (sampling) and how long to keep them (retention) are therefore design decisions with a price attached, and they belong in design review alongside the others.

**Cost as a fitness function.** The second move turns cost into something checked automatically, the way earlier chapters checked latency or coupling. Give each service a monthly budget. When a pull request changes the infrastructure, have continuous integration (CI) estimate what the change will add to the bill, so the reviewer sees a line such as "this adds a second database replica, roughly so many euros a month" next to the code. Then alert when actual spend drifts away from the budget. This is the book's usual governance pattern, a fitness function, measured in currency instead of milliseconds. Its real value is timing: it moves the conversation about cost from the end of the month, when the design is already running and expensive to undo, to the pull request, while the decision is still cheap to change.

**Capacity planning.** The third move concerns capacity, and here a common confusion needs untangling. Autoscaling is a mechanism: it adds instances when load rises. It is not a strategy, because it only reacts to load that has already arrived, and it cannot add capacity beyond the limits of whatever sits downstream. The strategy is to know your ceilings, the points where a component stops coping no matter how many copies of it you run (the order service's thread pool from Chapter 4 is one), to load-test up to those ceilings so they are measured rather than guessed, and to pre-scale ahead of the spikes you can foresee. Encore has an advantage here, because it schedules its own on-sales. Suppose a stadium tour goes on sale at 10:00 on a Friday. Operations knows that weeks ahead, so it warms the pools before 10:00 instead of letting autoscaling discover the crowd at 10:01. A city's transit authority works the same way: it adds trains by reading the fixture list, not by waiting until the stadium crowd is already packed onto the platform.

Pricing per invocation, the model of serverless functions described in Section 11.1, has a particular shape: the cost curve is the traffic curve. When nothing calls the function, it costs nothing; when traffic spikes, the bill spikes with it. That suits the Sale Gate, which idles between on-sales and then takes a burst of load for minutes. The same arithmetic runs in reverse for a workload that is busy all the time, because then it pays the per-call price on every call, every hour of the day, with no idle periods to save money in.

Encore's bill made this concrete. A metrics forwarder, a small component that relays monitoring datapoints to the observability backend, ran as a function invoked once per datapoint. That came to about 2,000 invocations a second, around the clock, or roughly 5 billion invocations a month. At list prices, the request fees plus 100 ms of 1 GB compute for each call came to about €9,000 a month, for work that never idled and so never benefited from the one thing per-invocation pricing does well. Moved to two small always-on containers that collect the datapoints and forward them in batches, the same work costs a few hundred euros a month.

It is worth being clear about how this happened, because it will happen again elsewhere. Nobody had been foolish. When the forwarder was small and its traffic was light and intermittent, a function was the obvious tool, and it was cheap. Then the traffic grew, gradually, past the shape the function was priced for, and no single month's growth looked alarming. The lesson is general: serverless fits work that is idle most of the time or arrives in spikes, and containers fit work that is busy and steady. The choice between them is arithmetic on the shape of the traffic, and it has to be redone as the shape changes, because arithmetic has no loyalties to the decision we made last year.

Sustainability, the carbon cost of what we run, mostly follows from the same efficiency. Right-sizing, which means running instances no larger than the work needs, cuts the energy wasted on idle cores. Spot capacity, the provider's spare machines sold at a discount on the condition that they can be reclaimed at short notice, puts otherwise idle hardware to use for interruptible work such as batch jobs. Choosing a region whose electricity is cleaner, where the latency to fans permits, lowers emissions directly. Each of these lowers the carbon and the bill together, which makes this one of the rare cases in engineering where virtue and economy point the same way.

**Recap.** We track cost per unit of business, such as cost per ticket sold, rather than total spend, and we watch line items like egress and telemetry that grow out of design. We estimate cost at review time and alert on drift, treating it as a fitness function like any other characteristic. Autoscaling is a mechanism, so it needs known ceilings and a calendar of foreseeable spikes behind it. The choice between serverless and containers is arithmetic on the shape of the traffic, to be redone as that shape changes, and sustainability mostly comes with efficiency.

**Exercise 11.4.** Define your product's unit cost, the spend divided by a business unit such as one order, one user-day or one API call. Compute it month by month over two months of billing and usage data. Then find the biggest swing in it and name the architectural event that explains it: a new service, a changed query, a logging change, a traffic shift or something else.

### 11.5 Operability by Design

Werner Vogels, describing Amazon's practice in a 2006 interview, summed it up in a phrase: "you build it, you run it." The team that writes a service is the team that gets woken when it fails. That arrangement changes what a team cares about while it is still designing, and it carries a corollary for architects. Whether a system is easy or miserable to run is not something operations can add later; it is decided at design time, by the same choices that decide availability or security. A service with no way to see inside it, or no known breaking point, was built that way, and no amount of heroism on call will fully make up for it.

**Production readiness** turns that corollary into a checklist that every service answers before its first deploy. Has the team declared its SLOs, so everyone knows what "working" means? Are the runbooks written, so whoever is paged at 3 a.m. knows what to do? Are the scaling ceilings known, so nobody discovers them during an on-sale? Are the dashboards wired, is a cost budget set, and has a backup been *restored*, not merely taken? That last item is the one teams most often skip, and the one that matters most. A backup that has never been restored is a hope with storage costs: until you have brought the data back and checked it, you do not know whether the files are complete, whether the restore takes ten minutes or ten hours, or whether anyone remembers how to do it. The checklist plays the same role in operations that Chapter 1's shortlist of driving characteristics plays in design. It names, in advance, the few properties that must hold, so that nobody has to remember them under pressure.

**Testing in the cloud** runs into a limit that every team meets sooner or later: a laptop is a poor imitation of production. The gap is widest for serverless code, because so much of its behavior comes from the platform around it. A function's real quotas, its concurrency limits, and the managed queues and databases it calls do not exist on a developer's machine, and an emulator can only approximate them. So we test in layers, each covering what the one before it cannot. *Contract tests* pin down what our code expects from each managed service and from the services it calls, and they run fast and early. *Ephemeral environments*, created for a pull request and torn down after it, exercise the real integration with real managed services. Section 11.2's *canaries* are the final check: a new version takes a small share of real traffic and is compared against the current one before it is promoted. Each layer catches what the earlier ones could not.

**Lifecycle maintenance** deals with a quieter kind of decay. Runtimes reach end of support, base images collect known vulnerabilities, and libraries drift out of date, all while the code that uses them stays unchanged. Software of this kind ages like fruit, not wine: left alone, it gets worse, and an upgrade postponed for two years is far harder than four small ones done along the way. Lehman's laws of software evolution, which Chapter 13 takes up, predict exactly this: a system in use must be continually adapted, or it becomes progressively less satisfactory. The practical consequence is that upgrades are scheduled work, with an owner and a place in the plan, like any feature. Chapter 15's platform automation will make them routine, but someone must own them even before that day arrives.

#### Surviving a regional outage {#surviving-a-region}

Most disaster recovery (DR) plans begin life as a document, and a document cannot decide anything. Two numbers turn DR from a document into architecture. The **recovery time objective (RTO)** is how long the business can tolerate being without the service before it returns. The **recovery point objective (RPO)** is how much recently written data the business can tolerate losing, measured as a span of time: an RPO of five minutes means that the last five minutes of writes may be gone after a disaster. Both are driving characteristics in Chapter 1's sense, but with a price tag attached, and the price climbs steeply. Each order of magnitude tighter, from hours to minutes, or from minutes to seconds, multiplies the cost, because each step demands more replicas, more automation and more coordination between sites.

Encore's own services show how differently those numbers can fall. The Catalog can be down for an hour and lose its last few minutes of edits; an organizer re-enters a changed venue description, and no fan is harmed. The seat ledger is another matter. It can be down only for minutes, and it must lose *nothing*, because every lost write there does real damage. If the ledger forgets that seat 14C was sold, it may sell 14C again, and two fans arrive at the same seat. If it forgets a reservation for which payment was taken, a fan has been charged for nothing. The ledger's demands therefore set the strategy, and the rest of the system follows. The tightest constraint is the one that sizes the design, in the same way that a bridge is built for the heaviest load it must ever carry, not for an ordinary Tuesday's traffic.

Chapter 4's consistency trade-offs tell us what each of the two broad strategies costs. In **active–passive**, one region serves all traffic and a replica in a second region follows along. This is the simpler and cheaper arrangement. If the replica is fed asynchronously, its RPO equals the replication lag: whatever the primary had written but not yet shipped when it failed is lost. Failover is then a rehearsed promotion of the replica to primary. In **active–active**, both regions serve traffic at once, which buys an RTO of seconds, because there is no standby to wake. The catch lies in writes that must be agreed across regions. If each write waits for the other region (synchronous replication), every write pays the cross-region delay. If it does not wait (asynchronous replication), two regions can accept conflicting writes to the same data, and someone must reconcile them later. No clever engineering removes that choice: the speed of light sets the delay, and the possibility of a network partition means a region cannot always tell whether its peer is alive. Encore takes a middle path. It runs active–active for reads, so a fan's seat map loads from the nearest region, and it *pins each event's writes to a home region*. This is Chapter 4's partition keys applied to geography: all the seat writes for a given concert go to one place, so they never conflict across regions.

That leaves one hard requirement: the seat ledger's zero RPO, even when a whole region is lost. Encore meets it with consensus. Each shard's ledger is a consensus group, running Chapter 4's Raft protocol with the same fenced terms, made of five voting replicas spread over three EU locations. Two sit in the event's home region, in different availability zones, and the leader normally lives there. Two sit in the other serving region. The fifth is a *witness* in a third region: it stores the replicated log and votes, but it serves no traffic. A write is acknowledged once three of the five replicas hold it durably. In practice, those three are the two home replicas plus whichever remote replica answers first. So every seat write pays exactly one cross-region round trip, about 18 ms between nearby EU regions. Group commit, which batches many reservations into each round of replication, keeps a shard at its ~1,500 ordered reservations a second despite that delay.

Why three locations and not two? Suppose the ledger lived in only two sites, and one of them fell silent. The survivor cannot tell whether its peer has crashed or the link between them has failed and the peer is still taking writes. It has only two options. It can wait for its peer, which means it stops writing and Encore stops selling seats. Or it can stop waiting and carry on alone, which quietly abandons zero RPO: every write it accepts now lives in one site only, and if the silent peer is in fact alive and doing the same, two leaders may sell the same seat. Nothing in a two-site arrangement can break that tie safely. A third location is what gives one side a majority, and a majority is what lets the system decide without guessing.

With three locations, the common failures become uneventful. If one node fails, the leader moves to its neighbor in the same region, and writes continue. If one network link fails, a majority can still be formed over the links that remain, so nothing changes for fans. The interesting case is the loss of the home region itself, or its isolation from both peers. Here the home region holds only two of the five votes, which is not a majority, so it loses its quorum. Its leader's lease and fencing token expire, and it stops accepting writes. Meanwhile the other serving region's two replicas and the witness hold three of five votes between them, so they elect a new leader in the other serving region. Two guarantees follow directly from the arithmetic. Every write that was acknowledged already sits on at least three replicas, so at least one of them is among the survivors, and nothing is lost. And because a minority can never commit, the isolated home region cannot keep selling seats on its own, so there is no split brain, no moment when two leaders both believe they own seat 14C. The ledger's minutes of RTO are spent on the election and on re-routing the Gate and Orders to the new leader.

The shape is a proven one, not an invention for this book: Google Spanner's multi-region configurations, Amazon DynamoDB's multi-Region strong consistency and Amazon Aurora DSQL all place a witness beside their serving regions. Its price is equally clear. Every seat write carries extra latency, and Encore has a third site to operate. Only the seat ledger pays that price, because only the seat ledger needs what it buys. The Catalog keeps its simpler asynchronous arrangement, with an RPO of a few minutes that nobody minds.

**Data residency** adds a constraint of a different kind: legal rather than technical. EU law restricts transfers of personal data out of the European Economic Area (EEA), and contracts or sector rules often go further and require that the data be stored inside the EU outright. For Encore, whose fans' names and email addresses are personal data, this means that the regions chosen for DR are not a free choice. Replicating fans' personal data to a cheaper region outside the EEA might be quick to build, but it could be unlawful to run. Residency therefore draws a context boundary along regulators' lines: data that must stay in the EU lives in services whose storage and replicas stay in the EU, and the boundary is drawn where the law requires it. Some of the most durable boundaries in a system are drawn by lawyers.

**Game days** are how all this design gets tested. A game day is a deliberate failover, staged on a quiet day with the team watching, and it is the fitness function for DR. It is needed because a failover you have never run is only a guess about how a failover would go. A game day cannot prove that recovery will work in every real disaster, since no rehearsal reproduces every circumstance. What it does reliably is show where recovery will *not* work: the runbook step that refers to a renamed dashboard, the credential that expired, the timeout set too low. That is the next best thing to proof. Encore drills quarterly, and the drill that went badly taught more than the three that went well.

The thesis of the chapter fits in one line: *operability is a property you design, then verify with fitness functions, like every other characteristic since Chapter 1.* If that is true, then the tools for verifying it should not be rebuilt by every team for itself. Golden pipelines, SLO tooling, cost dashboards and readiness checks should become a product that every team gets for free, and building that product is Chapter 15's subject.

**Recap.** Production readiness is a checklist answered before the first deploy, and it includes a backup restore that has actually been tested. Testing comes in layers, and canaries check against real traffic what local tests cannot. Upgrades are owned, scheduled work. RTO and RPO size the DR strategy, the tightest constraint governs it, and only game days prove it.

**Exercise 11.5.** Run the production readiness checklist against a service you own today: SLOs, runbooks, scaling ceilings, dashboards, cost budget and a tested restore. Each "no" is either this sprint's work or an accepted risk; write down which.

### Kata: The Locker Network

> **Your brief: "Relay."** Relay runs a parcel-locker network: 40,000 lockers across three countries. Each locker reports its door opens, parcel deposits and alarms over cellular links that drop out often. Around the lockers sit a courier app, a consumer pickup app and integrations with the carriers who deliver the parcels. Most of the time the lockers send a steady trickle of events, with peaks in the evening when people collect their parcels and 20× the usual load in Black Friday week. When a locker loses its connection, it stores its events and retries them in a batch once it is back online. So when a regional cellular outage ends, 3,000 lockers reconnect *at once*, each with a backlog to deliver. Relay has 18 engineers, complaints about the on-call load in two countries, and a cloud bill growing 8% a month while revenue grows 3%.

**Deliverables:**

1. **Execution-model map** — place each workload in a row of Figure 11.1: locker ingestion, the courier and consumer APIs, the carrier batch jobs and alarm processing. Name explicitly the design that absorbs the reconnection storm.
2. **Delivery design** — the GitOps layout of repositories and environments, how preview environments will work, and the canary policy for firmware-adjacent services, where a rollback is slow.
3. **SRE pack** — the SLI, SLO and error budget for pickup availability; the set of alerts that fire on symptoms; and the skeleton of a runbook for "regional cellular outage."
4. **Cost intervention** — Relay's unit cost, three hypotheses that could explain why spend grows 8% a month while revenue grows 3%, and the telemetry that would confirm or rule out each one.
5. **One architecture decision record (ADR)** — the buffer-and-shed design for the reconnection storm (queue the events that can wait, and deliberately drop the ones the system cannot absorb), stating in writing what it gives up.

**Rubric:**

| Criterion | The question to ask yourself |
|---|---|
| Shape matching | Does each workload's execution model match its traffic shape, and is the match backed by arithmetic? |
| Storm authorship | Is the reconnection of 3,000 lockers designed for in advance, or rediscovered in production every year? |
| Budget governance | Does the error budget, rather than a manager, decide when firmware canaries are promoted? |
| Unit economics | Is the cost divergence diagnosed against a business denominator, or explained by hand-waving? |
| Slow-rollback delivery | Do firmware-adjacent services get a canary policy that assumes a rollback takes days, and do preview environments work without real lockers? |
| Losses in ink | Does the ADR state which locker events may be delayed or dropped during the storm, and for how long? |

#### Where you now stand

You can now place each workload on the responsibility spectrum according to its traffic shape, deliver change through automated pipelines with git as the record, set reliability targets with arithmetic rather than hope, and read the cloud invoice as one more stream of telemetry about the architecture. Chapter 12 turns to systems whose central component is *probabilistic*: the same input can produce different outputs. There "correct" becomes a distribution of acceptable answers rather than a single expected one, testing becomes evaluation against samples, and natural-language input opens a new class of security risk.
